SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

TO DISTINGUISH IRON AND STEEL PIPE. Iron and steel pipe may be readily distin- guished by a flattening test, according to statemente made at the meeting of the Ameri- can Society of Heating and Ventilating En- gineers. Soft steel pipe, cat in very short Lengths or rings, flattens smoothly and evenly without breaking, while wrought iron pipe usually fractures at two or more places when flattened.

COLOURS THAT PRODUCE DARKNESS. If we look through a blue glass we see all objects blue, and if we look through a red glass we will see all objects red, but if we look throtigh both at the same time, and the blue and the red are pure colours, we will not see a combination of red and blue, that is, violet; but no light will pass at all, and the two classes together will look deep black. The reason is that ths blue glass allows no light to pass except blue, while the red glass allows none to pass except yed; the net re- sult being that nothing gets through at all.

" For about three years," said Miss Eva AL Fry, a clerk in the post office at 328, Hackney- road, London, "the pain I constantley bad at try chest was excruciating. I was afraid to eat my ' meals, because I was so stifled afterwards. I would have fits of dizziness, and darkness before my eyes that caused me great anxiety in carrying out my duties. WOMEN'S CHAT.

A Cabinet Minister's Wedding.—Mr. McKenna, the first Lord of the Admiralty, is the second Cabinet Minister in the present Ministry to marry during his term of wedding with Miss Jekyll is fixed for June 3rd, and will take place at St. Mergarets, Westminster, in the presence of Mr. and Mrs. Asquith, and a large circle of Parliarcentaty friends. In one respect Mr. Mc Kenna is more fortunate than the Lord Chancellor, who was married last autumn. lie can spend his honeymoon a here he likes, a privilege denied to the keeper of the Great Seal, the Lord

Chancellor, who is required never to leave ihe kingdom during his tenure of otlice. lle is supposed alwa3s to have the seal in his personal custody, and as this is required to be attired to high documents of State, the business of the realm would be brought to a standstill

during his absence abroad. Lady Irene Denison's Debut.-- Lord and Lady Londesborough's dinner and ball at St. Dunstan' s Lodge on June Ist, in honour of the debut oi Lady Irene Denison will rank as one of the great Society enter- tainments of the London season. The King and Queen will.honour the Earl and CounteEs with theis:, company _ .

ASBESTOS SLATES.

----- - According to a German publication, a firm in Munich has succeeded in artificially ren- dering asbestos waterproof, and has put upon the market this new kind of asbestos under the title of asbeetos slates, which are thus de- scribed: "These - asbestos slates, it is claimed, are as hard and as strong as the natural slates, and can therefore be laid on wall or roof constructions without any wooden laths being necessary." It is further claimed that they are a ',protection against fire, heat, cold, dampness, disease germs, and vermin.

ARE BACTERIA SEANTICEDSI

- A German investigator, Professor Dunbar, has published a paper in which he discusses- a theory of the origin of bacteria arising out of a large nienber of experiments which he has earrie€l out. These organisms have gene- rally been classified as a special group of the fungi, but Professor Dunbar is inclined to think that they are not an independent group at all, but rather certain stages in the life history of certain algae. The facts on which he baaes this conclusion are that when he made, with all due precautione as to sterilising, &c., pure cultures of algm, bac- i • teria appeared n them. The bacteria wero apparently derived from the algae.

69 YEARS A CLERGYMAN.

The death occurred on Sunday of the Rev. Angus Bethune, vicar of Seahrun, who was born in 1911, and discharged his clerical duties until a fortnight ago. Death was due to old age. The deceased cleric was 69 years a clergyman in the diocese of Durham, for 49 years was incumbent of cue parish, and served under seven Li.ihope of Durham.

EXTORTION FROM THE POOR.

" The act of a vampire," was the Liverpool Coroner's description of a money-lending transaction which was brought to his notice, on Tuesday in connection with the death of n woman named Ellen Ilrinkwortb, the wife of a dock labourer. Last Christmas she borrowed 22s Gd at interest of 2d. per shilling per week, and it • Was suggested that depression resulting from the burden of the debt had caused her to commit suicide. The rate of interest worlis out at SC6 per cent per annum. I The Coroner said 2d. per shilling a day was sometimes charged, and that was equal to O,COO per cent. per annum. The law, unfortunately, did not f.tet at money- lenders of this class—only public opinion could do so. I To the money-lender (a woman) the Coroner said," No wonder ycu look thriving." t

A MASTER IN PORTRAITURE. The exhibition of pictorial portrait work by Mr. Funky Lewis, which was opened last week at the rooms of the Royal Photographic Society, and which remains open until June 9th, free of charge on presentation of visiting card, is a most noteworthy show, end one whielf should be seen by everyone who can do so. OZOBROME IMPROVED. Mr. Thomas Manly, who is indefatigable in his efforts to perfect what has been called the evening carbon " 'process, has just completed experiments which have enabled him to produce a pigmenting solution and baths which will keep quite indefinitely., so that now the process becomes perfectly uniform 111 various countries and at all seasons. THE SOCIETY OF COLOUR PHOTOGRAPHERS. The second exhibitien of this society will be opened from JUDO Ist to June 27th at the little galleries of " The British Journal of Photography," Wellington Street, Strand, and although it is only six months since the last exhibition of the society, and in spite of the fact that those six months have been of the worst weather for autochrorne work, I know enough of the exhibits to be able to assure my readers that they show very great strides in the use of the autochrome plat . RINETOGRAPHY ON DRY PLATES. Amethod of photographing "living " pictures on circular dry plates is being boomed jest now as a novelty apparently by people who forget that six or eight years ago the kammatograph camera was placed on the market and did this very thing efficiently. By using very small pictures, and by moving the circular plate in such a way that the pictures form a series from the centre to the circumference, it is possible to get a thousand subjects on a glass plate of something under sixteen inches diameter, hut a thousand pictures is only equiva- lent to a relatively very short roll of kinetogiaph films, and the pictures are necessarily so small that they will not give brilliant projections on anything like the same sesle as can be obtained with the cialinary standard films.

THE PHOTOGRAPHIC CONVENTION. The Photographic Convention is drawing very near,and I am glad to know that it's first departure from British shores seems likely to be signalised by a record attend- ance of British members. At the Council meeting the other night, the secretary, Mr. F. A. Bridge, was able to announce that he bed already booked 35 new members in addition to receiving renewal subscriptions from a very great number of old ones, and I believe it 'a a fact that never in the history of the Convention have so many new members been booked so long before the , meeting. The arrangements are most efficient down to the smallest detail. The President, Sir E. Cecil Hertslet , who is the British representative in Antwerp, has taken an enormous amount of trouble to make the visit a success and all the local people have exerted themselves

I M - CONVENTION BUGBEARS In spite of the fact that a larger number of members have joined the Convention this year than have been booked at the corresponding period in any previous year, there are still some enquirers who seem to hesitate because of their fear that Belgium is very far away from England. As a matter of fact, the methods of teaching Brussels are quick, convenient, and reasonable in price. For those who dread the sea trip, there are passages reduced to about forty minutes, while there who are reasonable sailors can greatly enjoy I the passages from the East Coast and the North country ports, in splendid boats, which sail at convenient hours at night, and land their passenger about breakfast-time the next morning. All the boat companies have made special arrangements for return fares,which are unusually low, on condition that the members travel outward in parties, but allowing them to return individually at any time during a couple of months or so. For residents in the North of England and Midlands, the recently established Great Central service from Grimsby is specially convenient, while those living near London and in the South and \Vest of England have the choice of many different routes, of which the favourite seem to be the Great Eastern by Harwich, end the South Eastern and Belgian State Bailways by the Dover- Ostend route.

FASHIONS AND MORAL COURAGE.

The fashions would never alter, declares Ella Hepworth Dixon in the Lady's Pictorial, if women were not people with infinitely more moral courage than men. To all practical pur- poses men-folk wear so-day the same garments which their forebears wore about 1848, when frills and fancy waistcoats were definitely out, and the reign of Pure Reason—as they used to say in the French Revolution—came in. A certain modern poet with hyacinthine locks used to declare that it required more courage to wear your hair long in England than to face a Mauser rifle at SCO yards. Yet what feminine person hesitates to dress her hair in some new and alarming fashion, to decorate it with fruit or strange fowl, . even if she knows that every head will be turned .to look at her? What man, one may ask, would i have the courage to wear a vast hat covered ' with a parterre of brilliant-hued flowers, a hat, moreover, which rested on his shoulders at the back? Who, among the stronger-minder sex, would one year wear a skirt distended like a hoop, and the next a garment so attenuated as to disclose every line of his form? True moral courage consists in braving public opinion, in venturing to disregard the hostile criticism of the small boy in the street. For, in the last analysis, it is the small boy in the street who is the arbiter of that delicate matter our personal appearance.

A DAINTY NIGHTGOWN.

Pictured in our illustration is a particularly pretty and dainty nightgown of rather novel de- sign. It is realised in the very finest of nain- sook, and is quite an ideal garment for summer wear. This "nightie" is slightly decolleto at the top, and is finished by a frill of lace and e. band of ribbon-threaded embroidery. Below this

embroidery comes a band of the material embroi- dered with large French knots, and below again a band of insertion, which in front develops into a long pointed tab. Two other similarly shaped tabs spring from the circular band of insertion on either side, the spaces between being filled with tiny pin tucks. Wide bell sleeves, finished with a frill of lace, French knots, and a band of insertion, complete this pretty model.

_ ARTIFICIAL FLOWERS.

The use of artificial flowers has been greatly extended, says the Queen, for many people wear them by day, and never was the millinery more overloaded with them; but they are wonderfully made, it is almost impassible to tell them from the real thing, except when they assume un- natural colours. Gardenias, violets, and roses are wonderfully natural; orchids can be most faithfully copied; and so successful has the arti- ficial florist been, that many large ballrooms and reception-rooms have the artificial blooms mingled with real foliage, and defy detec- tion. Real flowers occupy the lower portion of the room, the artificial the higher.

TO-DAY'll RECIPE.

FISII AND MACARONI PlE—Fish and macaroni pie makes an excellent luncheon or supper dish, and any kind of fish may be used for it. Re- move all the skin and bone from half a pound of cold cooked fish, and break it into large flakes. Break a quarter of a pound of macaroni into Pieces about half an inch long, throw it into a pan of fast - boiling water, add one tablespoonful of butter, cook till tender. Butter a pudding- did', put in a layer ot fish, then some pieces of butter, sprinkle with two tablespoonfuls of grated cheese, and a little salt and pepper. Now nut in a layer of macaroni, then some but- ter, cneese, and so on, nu 1/10 dun 13 tun. Sprinkle a thick layer of grated cheese on the top. Put some piecea c f butter here and there on it, and bake in a hot oven till it is a nice brown colour. Serve very hot.

I know that very soon I shall as comfortable as possible. Worthington gave have ceased to exist in this world and be with , them brandy, but they still shivered with the 1851115321812615 maNZECOMMESUIC Alice again." cold, in spite of their warm wraps and great- . coat* Most of the men were absolutely silent.

They seemed stunned with the horror of the perils through which they had passed. But every now and then one of them would look back at the ship they were leaving, as it lay a broken- backed and forlorn Wreck amidst a smother of foam, and make a queer foreign gesture or a cluck of pity with the tongue. Most silent of all was the captain, a queer, wizened old fellow,. with grey-whiskered and wind-tanned, wrinkled face. He sat huddled up on his seat, his head miserably buried in his hands. Occasionally in a hoarse and broken voice he muttered to himself in his native tongue: "My ship—my good ship-- I have lost my ship." Worthington did everything he could to com- fort the poor old man; but he refused both sym-, pathetic words and brandy with emphatic nods. and then buried his face again in his hands. Meanwhile the lifeboat was making apparently but little headway against a head sea. Great waves buffeted her, and then raced past, and Were lost in the morning mist. But all the crew were at the oars, and in reality their sturdy efforts were making considerable progress. -Al- ready the Henrik Peterson was fast _fading from view, and the tall cliffs of Barham Point were hardly to be distinguished from the rest of the coast, All of a sudden the little wizened captain lifted up his head, as if to take one last look at his ship. The men rowing near him noticed that his eyes filled with tears, and then, before any- one could guess what he was going to do, he rose to his feet, and plunged over the side of the vessel into the sea. There was a moment of eonsternation. The action was so utterly unexpected. Then Craske gave a short, sharp order, and the lifeboat swung slowly round. But before the manceuvro was exeeuted a figure followed the Norwegian into the sea. "It's the doctor," said James Lake, with an oath. "Pull, lads; but I'm afeaed we shan't reach him." There was the short, choppy swell that always- follows a big sea, while every now and then a huge roller would come thundering along in its course to the beach. By the time the lifeboat had turned, the two men in the water were al- ready nearly a hundred yards away. The Nor- wegian was still swimming, as if he meant to reach the vessel which they had just left. But he was obviously growing more and more tired, and was now only borne along like a piece of driftwood on the waters. Worthington had nearly overhauled him. The boat seemed to move desperately slowly, although the men rowed as if their backs would crack. But there was every hope that, if Worthington could sup- port the captain in the water for a few moments, they would be able to rescue them both. A great wave smote the stern of the boat, making her tremble all over, knocking the oars out of several of the men's hands, and drenching every- thing in a cloud of blinding spray. When it had passed, and they could see again, both the swim- mers had disappeared. Their bodies were washed up au the shore two days later. [THE END.]

BRIQUETTES FOR ENGINES.

Briquettes for locomotive fuel are being ex- perimented with by the Rock Island Railroad Company. The binder used is water gas pitch, and in the various experiments 6,7, and 8 per cciat. of the binder has been used. In manu- facturing the 'briquettes the coal and pitch are ground up separately, and then mixed in the proper proportions. From the storage bin the coal passes into steam jacketed conveyorsbheated by superheated steam, and the hot product passes through the briquetting machine, which compresses the briquettes at the rate of from five to eight tons per hour. It is found better to cool them by passing them through water to induce quick setting. • BY GIANT LIFT.

Facility of locomotion is a watchword of the times, and what with electrie trains and tram- ways and other motor vehicles, engineers are seeing to it that we get about as easily as possible. Hills are, of course, a chief obstacle

INSTEAD OF CLIMBING HILLS.

tri the way of mechanical traction of all kinds. For cities or towns that are vctholly or in part built upon high hills the giant electric lift, which we depict, is now being made use of. As will be readily understood, the passengers enter the car of the lift from the lift station, and are at once taken up perpendicularly to the height of the eminence to be gained. Along a light bridge the -car then travels to the higher parts of thei town, where, the hill ascended with- out, as t were, any climbing, the passengers alight. Stockholm, the principal streets of which are built on rising ground, has one of these giant lifts in operation.

WARRINGTON MUSICAL FESTIVAL.

A GREAT SUCCESS.

Saturday was a day to be long and well remembered in Warrington for it witnessed the bolding of the first Warrington Musical Festival— the precursor, it is hoped, of many sinalar annual gatherings for the advancement of musical culture. The promoters are to be congratulated upon the success of their initial enterprise. It speaks much for, the attractiveness of the programmes and the widespread interest aroused, that audiences could be found to fill the Parr Hall - on an ideal May day when it was delightful to be out of doors. Even in the afternoon most parts of the hall were comfortably filled whilst in the evening the ; building was crowded to the doors. Although this was the first Musical Festival it was by no means the first competitive gathering that Warrington haehad, and the present is an opportune time to pay a tribute to the ; persevering and disinterested efforts of the Apollo . Choir who fur several years organized an annual , Eisteddfod, not without loss to themselves. It was, however, felt that the responsibility ought no longer to rest upon the shoulders of so small a circle and towards the end of last year a strong committee of ; townsmen was formed, with the Mayor as President, 1 to take over the enterprise and extend its operations. I The result is that the Warrington Musical Festival has taken the place of the Eisteddfod and if the enthusiasm which has so far claaracterised the workers continues, it should in time rank high amongst the many festivals which are now held in this country. Satur- day's attendances showed that the sympathy of a large body of the inhabitants has been enlisted, while the high talent attracted wasm-guarantee or success from the competitive standpoint. The programme at each concert was nicely varied. In the afternoon children's contests were to the fore and there was ample evidence of the good which these festivals can accomplish in cultivating ,the nausicid talents of the rising generation. The action song was a delightful feature and added a dash of colour to the proceedings which the audience was not slow to appreciate. We hope to see this feature become even more popular. The children's choral competition was entered into with keen enthusiasm and it was good to hear the fresh young yeaces_jeining in pleasing folk-songs. Some disappointment- -IC-de-felt that •only,one choir the Manchester Orpheus —put in an appearance-for the second choral competition, but it was more than compensated f.r by the magnificent rendering which this famous choir gave of the test piece. The Mayor showed his desire to encourage the festival by presiding over the afternoon meeting i and deliver- ing a few homely observations n which he specially commended the good work which was being done amongst the children. The Mayoress distributed, the prizes. The evening concert provided ace of the richest musTcal treats ever afforded a Warrington audience. The chief choral competition attracted no fewer than eight leading mixed choirs of the north and there was a battle royal for the prizes. It was an education in itself to hear some of these choirs sing the beautiful test pieces "He, watching over Israel" and "Moonlight," and far from the repetition palling, the music seemed to grow on one and fresh gems of harmony were ever being brought into prominence. When at the close of the contest Dr: McNaught con- ducted the massed choirs while they again sang the test pieces the effect was grand and inspiring The success of Mr. Harry Berrey's choirs from St. Helens and Runcorn proved most popular and they are to be congratulated upon triumphing in a competition where the talent was remarkably high. The contest for local church and chapel choirs aroused keen interest and it was gratifying to hear the adjudicator congratu- lating Warrington upon having four such excellent choirs. Mr. Frank Hickman 's Bold-street Choir achieved a notable and well-earned victory. The festival promoters were favoured with the presence of Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., and Mrs. Crosfield who have been generous supporters of the undertaking and must have been pleased to see what a fine competition there was for the shield which Mrs. Crosfield presented for the chief event. Mr. Crosfield delivered a brief but well chosen speech. The committee were very fortunate in their choice of adjudicators, Dr. W. G. McNaught, Mr. Wilfred Jones and Miss A. Backsheen Wood dis- charging these duties to the satisfaction of all. Dr. McNaught is a host in himself, for while carefully weighing the merits and demerits of the respective competitors, his adjudications were seasoned with touches of humour, and delivered in the happiest and kindest way. He had a capable colleague in Mr. Wilfred Jones, while Miss Backsheen Wood did good service by judging the violin solo competition. At evch concert the a adience had the pleasure of hearing a song by Miss Frances Andrews (contralto), and a violin solo by Miss Backsheen Wood, both of whom are artistes of repute, and added much to the enjoy- ment of the festival by their contributions. Miss Andrews sang "Sincerity " (Clarke) and " The Flower Song" (Gounod) with pleasing expression, while Miss Backsheen Wood's violin solos " Dance of Gypsies " (13aeltez) and " Tamboarin Dance " (Solvate) evidenced that fine technique and musicianly taste which had previously gained berconsiderable popularity with Warrington concert-goers. Mr. E. Lloyd-Jones, as chairman of the restiv:sl Committee, conducted each meeting. The pianoforte accompaniments were tastefully played by Mr. W. a. Payton, Mus. Mac. and Mr. E. Wilkes.

Praise is clue to the festival officers for the admir- able arrangements. They were : Mr. E. Lloyd Jones, chairman; Mr.Nath a n Cook,vice-chai rrnan ; committee, Messrs. R. H. Albery, J. Broadhurst, H. Bond, T. Brough,W.Critchlev, J. Corrin, W. Clay, 0.11. Dawson, A. Greenough, W.-Hipkiss, S. Hassan, Fl. 0. Hughes, E. Iddop, W. Knowles,F. E. Penketh,W. H. Payton F. Pendlebury, G. Ratcliffe, P. L. RI ' chardson A. Spruce, W. T. Williams, W. Woodcock and E. Wilkes ; treasurer, Mr. Frank Hickman ; assistant secretary, Mr. P. J. George ; general secretary, Mr. It. Cook. Special mention must be made of the work of Mr. R. W. Cook, the capable secretary, who has been untiring in his efforts to promote the success of the festival—in fact the duties could not have been in better hands.

AFTERNOON CONCERT. There was a large attendance at the afternooc con- cert, the Mayor (Alderman Smetburst),yresiding.

WELCOME TO THE MAYOR. Mr. E. LLOYD Jos said that when they called a town's committee together to take over the Eisteddfod of the Apollo Choir, they felt they' could not do better than have the Mayor at the head of the festival. They hoped he would be Mayor next year and also President of next year's festival. (Applause). The Mayon said he felt proud to be the President of the Warrington Musical Festival' on what might be termed its birthday. He believed the festival would do a great amount of good in training young people to sing God's praises. There was nothing so beautiful in their churches and chapels as good music. He hoped that as the result of the education imparted at musical festivals there would be none of the terrible discords they heard sometimes. (Laughter). lie wished the movement every success. (Applause). PIANOFORTE kOLO COMPETITION (AGE NOT TO EXCEED T WEL There were nineteen entries for this competition, the test piece being" Scherzo, Op 195 " (Giulia). Before delivering his adjudication, Dr. McNaught said he was delighted to he in Warrington again and to see so much interest taken in that movement. He hoped the festival would grow until it was as large as anything of the kind in the country. (Applause.) Referring to the pianoforte solo competition, he said it was quite right that a simple piece should be chosen. It was wrong to ask young people to attempt a piece that was beyond their technical capacity. H players were extra good they: could always show their artistic touch and execution in a simple piece. In this contest, he awarded the first prize (10s. ad.) to Emily Woolley, of Runcorn, who bad a very nice touch—not very firm, but as she was young there was plenty of time to got it tight—and a pleasant, dainty style of playing. The accentuation was capital. There were one or two slips in the left hand, but she was a very promising player and one thing he 'Ned about her performance was that she was able to work up gradually to a climax. The second prize went to J. Birchall, St. Helens, a boy who showed true musical faculty, crispness of touch and a sense of accent. More delicacy would have been welcome. but it was a very picturesque performance.

CHALLENGE SOLO- FOR GIRLS AND DOI'S (UNDER 16 YEARS OF AGE.) In this competition twenty-one young vocalists sang solos of their own choice. Mr. Wilfred Jones adjudi- cated and said that it had been a very good competi- tion. In awarding the first prize (10s.) to Hobert Hamer, of Accrington, he said he had a delightful

VIOLIN SOLO COMPETITION. (FOR SCHOOL CHILDREN.) This was a pleasing feature of the festival and attracted eighteen entries. The test piece was "Cambria "—an arrangement of Welsh airs by Mareston. The winner of the first prize (10s.) was Fred Brough, of Nutgrove, whose artistic playing of the piece from memory quite delighted the audience. Mies Backsheen Wood adjudi- cated and said the winner displayed real temperament and had sufficient techingne to mak4 the violin say what he meant. The second prize (55.) went to W. Swain, of Warrington, who the adjudicator said showed some promise and played intelligently, but he did not make the phrases clear. J. Edmondson, of Tyldesley, was awarded the third prize, his rendering being very fair, but not always in tune. CHILDREN'S ACTION SONO. . .

This item proved exceptionally interesting, although only two parties competed—Stockton .Heath Band of Hope (conducted by Mrs. Whitruore) and Brunswick Sunday School, Bury, conducted by Miss Platt. The children were prettily costumed, and Stockton Heath sang about the sad fate of a butterfly, whilst Brunswick gave a sprightly gipsy song. Dr. McNaught said he was a great believer in the educational value of action songs. They taught a great deal more than those who had not thought about it had any idea of—the poetry of motion, the power of doing things in unison and graceful movement. In giving an action song the com- petitors ought to see that the action started from the time they appeared on the platform and continued until they disappears I. There should be no cessation of appeal to the eye and to the ear. The Stockton Heath children were nicely costumed, and there was a pretty contrash of colour. They were also well drilled, but there was a stop during which they were quite still, and there was some uncertainty about the exit. Tho action had point in it and was done with unanimity. The tune itself was rather angular but the singing was good. Ile awarded them 60 marks. The Brunswick party gave a more experienced performance. They at once impressed the audience by an effective entry. The costumes were good and the colours were brilliant. The pace was a trifle slow and the dancing should be lighter. The performance presented a delightful spectacle and there was no pause, something of interest and great varietyl marking it throughout. He awarded them 75 marks and they therefore obtained the prize of two guineas. CHILDREN'S CHOILIL COMPETITION. Five choirs appeared in this contest, the test pieces being" Whispering Wind" (Labhett) and " Oft in the stilly night" (MeNaught). Considerable interest contred in the efforts of two local choirs—Warrington Band of Hope Union and Beamont Council School. Dr. McNaught remarked that some of the choirs had a difficulty in singing in tune. Without entering into a detailed criticism he awarded the marks as follows : Sutton United Juvenile Choir (conductor, Mr. W. Knapper), 146 ; Warrington Band of Hope Union Select I Choir (Mr. A. Spruce), 142; Beamont Council School, Warrington (Mr. NV. 51ason) 122 ; Eldon-street Council I School, Preston (Mr. W. Crozier), 122 ; Rhyl-street Mission Juvenile Choir, Bootle (Mr. J. Roberts), 97. The first prize of £3 ard silver medal to each member of the successful choir went to Sutton United and the second prize of £2 to Warrington Band of Hope.

He painted largely, but he did not paint a word so much as a phrase or an idea. His voice was somewhat tight and might be loosened in a certain way to make it luller,richer, rounder and more resonant. Miss Kenworthy had a good sive. Dr. MoNaught in delivering the adjudication said voice but she indulged in too much portamento. Her they had had some remarkable singing. He and enunciation and intonation were interfered with by that! his colleague were perfectly agreed on the result. The one fault. Blackpool Choir gave a very good performance but the Ifirst chorus was rather rigid and too square. So far as technique was concerned he enjoyed it, but from the point of view of expression, tenderness and sympathy he did not enjoy it so much. The choir was well in tune but it was not a moving performance. In the rendering of "Moonlight" there was unity and the choir showed that it was well disciplined. Some parts were very well done indeed but other chances were missed and on the whole the interpretation was not expressive. The Stretford Glee and Madrigal Society were a wee bit slow in the first chorus. The tenors in this choir were a great asset. The choir sang very well but the performance was not moving. The end was treated more like a part song with its dilly-dally ralleatando and he did not think that piece bore it—it was too great for that. The performance needed the aurora of inspiration. In the pat t song the warm tone was an attractive feature. The piece was taken too quickly at times. The closing passages were charming and if they had sung the whole of the part song as well as they sang the end, they would have been more triumphant. Runceirn and Widnes Choral Society gave a remarkable performance. The tone and style were perfect but above all that there was the soul speaking to a soul and that moved them rill. It certainly moved him intensely and he did not think he had heard the chorus sung much better in his life. The choir had an exceedingly skilful conductor who showed inspiration. In "Moonlight " there was con- spicuous unity and beautiful tone, There was broad colouring and yet the innar colouring was beautiful as well and there was a plastic rhythm throughout. With regard to the Preston Choir, the pace and tone were good but it was not quite so warm—he supposed the impression of the other choir was still in their ears. The performance was not deeply moving and if that chorus was not moving it had missed its fire. The gradations were exaggerated but there were many points in the performance which he admired. In the St. Helens Choir they again had those fine ideas. The con- ductor, he supposed, was the same as the Runcorn choir's. (Applause.) He was, therefore, trying to beat himself and he (Dr. McNaught) did not know that he entirely succeeded in the first chorus. Still it was a splendid performance. The conductor had the same perfect control. He held the choir in the hollow of hi a hand and did what be likad with them. ft was a beautiful plastic material that he had to deal with. All the technical points were almost perfect but the end rallentando, he submitted meekly, was overdone. Their rendering of eC Moonlight " was quite fascinating. The conductor had such a splendid machine in front of him and he played on it sdith rare art. There was just enough movement and portamento to charm and the performance was one of the finest he had heard in his life. The Earlestown Choir was a young one and he was glad to welcome them. These competitions were not only for the best—if they were they were beginning to fail,—because they wanted to encourage the younger Choirs. (Applause). If the competition had not been of such a high standard this choir would have done remarkably well. There was a fervent attack—too fervent for this chorus—but they were enthusiastic—a quality which later on would be utilized to good advantage. There were many evidences of great earnestness and conscience but they were pitted against such remarkably good ckoirs that they could not expect to come near victory at present. They, however, had their time before them. With regard to the Salford choir, the greatest impression he had of their performance was the neatness and precision—it was so well groomed all through and beautifully clear. It was not, however, such an impressive interpretation. lie admired it immensely as a bit of choral drill. In the pal song the tone was almost sleek ; the blend was perfect. The rhythm and accent were excellent but still he did not feel touched. His adjudicatorial soul was not moved. (Laughter.) lie had got a soul although he was an adjudicator, and he had to have a resaarkakly thick skin too. (Laughter.) The singing of the Southport choir was magnificently clear. They had fine basses, magnificent altos, beautiful sopranos, very good tenors. The pace seemed to be too great—at least it sounded hurried.. They had resources for the magnificent climax of the chorus but the highest expression was not quite realized. In the part song the basses were a tremendous asset in the blend. The end was beautiful—in fact it was the best ending, although on the whole it was not the finest perforroance. The marks were awarded as follows :—St. Helens, chorus ! 75, part song 78, total 153; Runcorn and Widnes. chorus 75, part song 76, total 151 ; Southport. chorus 73, part song 74, total 147; Preston' chorus 70, part song 74„ total 141; Stretford, chorus 69, part song 72, total 141; Salford, chorus 69, part song 71, total 140; Blackpool, chorus 69, part song 70, total 189; Earlestown, chores 55, part sow, 61, total 119. The victory of Mr. Berrey's choirs was 'loudly acclaimed by the audience. The challenge shield had not been completed but Mrs. Cros- field handed a copy of the design and the prize money to Mr. Berrey.

He really I thought she had rather shirked her duty in not doing 1 pleasureso. she felt i hnobew:nags allowedre 9 requested to pt roe sgeinv te them a little message which his wife might better have! conveyed herself. Se asked him to say what a great 1 ' a shield to the successful choir in that splendid corn- petition—(hear, hear)—and he hoped they would not I think it inappropriate that she should make the presenta- tion for two reasons. In the first place,as he had just told I them and as he thought some of those present already : knew, she was very fond of music and the other reason ! 1 : was that while by birth and by a recent little event—a leap 1 in the dark which might have been rather rash on her part 1 j —(laughter)—they could claim her for Lancashire, after ! 1 all her ancestors were in Greece and it was in Greece that i ' they found the earliest beginnings of the old chorus from 1 ! which had sprung that splendid development of choral I ' music which formed so great a part in the national 1 , music in these days in our country. (Applause.) Mrs. ! Crosfleld was anxious that the shield should if possible . be a link between the ancient musical ,tradi- tions of the chorus in the old Greek tragedies ; and the music of Lancashire which they were 1 celebrating that day,and she had designed what he hoped : would prove an interesting kind of shield as a trophy 1 for that occasion. It was taking some time to work 1 out, and he could only hope that the result would be 1 such as in the opinion of the successful choir might I justify a little delay. He wished they could have had ; the trophy ready for the festival, but his wife thought i it was better that it should be carefully done so as to ' carry out the ideas he had mentioned. (Applause.) He ! had referred to the extraordinarily important part which 1 music played in the education and national history of , the ancient Greeks, and they all knew that throughout ! the whole of the records of civilized history they could ! trace the striking part that music had played whether, in time of ear or of peace, and the influence it had had I upon national character. He had often thought it would I be interesting to have a comparison drawn between the 1 relative influences that various forms of art had exer- cised on different nations—architecture, sculpture, paint- ; ing, and last but far from least because it was I the most important of all, music. In time of war I they all knew the extraordinary influence that martial and national music exercised over people. 1 And then in the happier times of peace they realized I the effect of a Wagner overture or the Hallelujah Chorus. He had a relative who said he never went to hear" The Messiah " without feeling his hair lift a bit when the Hallelujah Chorus came on. (Laughter.) Although that evening they had not had any of blandel he would like to say parenthetically that in these com- plicated and difficult days when people turned to the , sublime beauty of Beethoven, the rather sombre pessimism of Brahros,the wierd melody of Chopin and to so many other composers of great note and ability, it was a very healthy thine' to turn back to those times when the world was a bit younger and have a little of the cherry optimism that rang through old Handel. (Applause.) Speaking for himself, he must confess that stirring as Handel's oratorios were, magnificent immortal as much was that he had written' in oratorio music what came most to his (the speaker's) heart were the most beautiful pieces of Mendelseohn, works like" Elijah," a chorus from which they had listened to with such great delight that evening. (Applause.) He could only in conclusion congratulate the visiting choirs on their splendid rendering of the test pieces. He was sure the audience very heartily thanked them and promised them as warm, perhaps even a warmer welcome if it were possible when they came to War- rington next year. (Applause.) SECOND MORA G COMPETITION.

This contest for male choirs attracted only two entries—Manchester Orpheus Glee Society and Wigan Harmonic Male Voice Choir. Wigan did not put in an appearance and Manchester Orpheus sang the test piece " King of Worlds " (Din d Jambi) and received half the prize—£6. - Conducted by Mr. W. S. Nesbitt this famous choir gave a most inspiring interpretation which called forth warm appreciation.

P ES DISTRIBUTED.

At the close of the competitions the prizes were dis- tributed by the illayoress. A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the Mayor and Mayoress on the proposition of Mr. 11. NV. Cook.

EVENING CONCERT. The hall was crowded in every plrt for the evening concert. Mr. A. LI. Crosfteld M.P., presided and was accompanied by Mrs. Crosfleld. The Member and his wife had a very cordial greeting on entering the ball and they took seats in the centre gallery to listen to the competitions. PIANOFORTE SOLO COMPETITION (12 TO IS YEIRS OF ACE) There were eighteen entries for this competition, the test piece being "Butterfly" (Greig). Dr. MoNaught said the playing of the finalists was remarkably good and reflected great credit upon the teachers. The piece was a well-known one, being delicate and pretty. He awarded the prizerof £1 ls. to Miss Doris Grosse,of Sale with 74 marks. The other competitors were placed as follows: —III iss F.Bennet t, 73; Master Charles Eckersley, 73; Rosalind " (St.lleleris), 60. SOPRANO SOl.O COMPETITION. The song chosen for this competition was" Magic Month of May " (Newton) and there were 24 entries. Dr. McNaught remarked that we ought to be able to sing the song very sympathetically in this country, for there was a great deal of magic about our May—more magic than poetry. (Laughter.) The song was a trying one in some respects as it oalled for piquant style, good compvs and light, dainty rhythm. , A dramatic soprano could not very well deal with it. He awarded the prize of £l. Is. to Miss Eva. Shaw, of Oldham, remarking that her's was a rather restricted production, something hindering the full flow of the voice. On the other hand, however, there was a dainty style and piquancy and she brought intelligence to bear on the performance. Her enunciation was good and the expression was well interpreted. He awarded her 73 marks. The other finalists were placed as follows.:—Miss A. Woodland, of Warrington, 64 ; Miss Howard, of Newton, 70 ; Miss Emily Tegg, of Manchester, 72. .Dr. McNaught said Miss Woodland's voice had is good, telling tone but the I enunciation WAS not well controlled and the rhythm was deficient in depth and delicacy. Miss Howard had a bright voice and a piquant style. Her scale singing was very fair but, she did not always produce a pleasant note. She; however, caught a great deal of the geniality of the composer. Miss Tegg forced her voice some- times but it was often of a very pleasant quality and she was able to impart mood and-td vary the tone. Her's was the best conception of the song but the inequalities of her voice did not enable her to give full vent to her feeling.

DUET COMPETITION. Nine couples entered for this competition and sang " Maying " (Smith.) The prize of 5:1 10s. was awarded to MISS Lily Fairhurst and Mr. Walter , Shaw. Dr. McNaught said the ensemble was excellent, both being highly temperamental singers, the baritone especially I so. He had a little way of sinking too low with his • voice, but it was a fine cultured style. There was a great deal to admire in the lady's performance, but she should not take so much care to make the verbal emphasis on wrong syllables. There was a tender, cooing note all through the piece, and they knew how to make the rebate. Miss Ethel Parr (St. Helens) and Mr. Walter Clay (Warrington) also bad beautiful voices, , -and the ensemble was capital. Their rendering showed ; an artistic style, but at times it was rather gloomy. I With regard to Miss Silcock and,Mr. J. E. Hannan (of ; Warrington), the young lady had an extremely beautiful voice, and sang with greater refinement than the gentle- man. If she developed her style the would make a very good name as a singer some day. The pair, however, were not well matched. The marks were allotted as follows :—Miss Fairhurst and Mr. Shaw, 71 ; Miss Parr and Mr. Clay, 73 ; Miss Silcock and Mr. Hannan, 69. Dr. McNanght said he was so charmed %iith the perform- ance of a boy and girl from Liverpool, who cotild not rightly be included ins competition against adults, that he had decided to give them a prize on his own account.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Golborne-street Baptist Church. The Sunday Schoo'l anniversary sermons were preached at this church on Sunday morning and evening by the Pastor, the Rev. R. Letheren. In the morning the rev. gentleman dealt with Sunday school work, and likened the Sunday school to a garden. It ought to be a place of service, fellowship, worship, safety. in the evening the preacher took for his text the words "And Jesus called a little child unto him, and set him in the midst of them." In the afternoon a scholars' service was held, over which Dr. Ferguson presided. Councillor Ball gave a very helpful address to the parents,teachers and scholars. At all the. services special hymns and choruses weie rendered by the children and friends under the conductorship of Mr. C. 11. Duwson, with Miss Ilankey as organist. The collections throughont the day, which were in aid of Sunday school funds realized slightly over £l5.

===== 0002601_19080530_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
Cairo-street Chapel.

Services in celebration of the Sunday school anni- versary were held on Sunday. The Rev. George A. Payne, of Knntsford, preached to large congregations at the morning and evening services, and his discourses were listened to with greatattentiou. In the afternoon Mr. Charles A. Pemberton gave an address. At all the services special anthems and hymns were rendered by the choir. Satisfactory collections were made.

STOCKTON HEATII.

ODDFELLOWSUIP.—The monthly meeting of the u Travellers Rest" Lodge was held at the Mulberry Tree ' Hotel, Stockton Heath, on Saturday evening, Noble 1 Grand Ilog,m, in the chair. P.G.M. Lot Brooks said he was glad to see the progress made by the Lodge since their removal to new rremises. Ho trusted they would still increase in numbers. P.D.G.M. G. F. Freeman remarked that the lodge was now on a sound footing and everything pointed to success nnmerically and financially. P.P.G.M. Frank Webster alluded to the proposed State pensions, and said the age limit was too old. The friendly societies had done yeomen service in providing payment in case of sickness and death. ' (Applause.) P.P.G.M. T. P. Bradshaw endorsed all that ; had been said and referred to the Lancashire and Cheshire Conference which the District Officers and Delegates had attended at Manchester in the afternoon. He hoped to be successful in his election on the Executive next year having only been defeated this year by three votes.

TENOR SOLO COMPETITION. Out of 16 competitors, C. M. Evans, of Liverpool, on! H. Phipps, of Birkenhead, were elected to sing "-My hope is in the Everlasting (Stainer) before (ho audience. MIA MISSION

Young Woman Found in Awful Con- dition with Scabies—Body a Mass of Sores from Scratching—Tried Many Remedies for Seven Weeks —Result Was Discouraging, But

===== 0002601_19080530_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
ITCHING TORTURES YIELDED TO CUTICURA

"While I was doing missionary work In the lower portion of several cities I found it necessary to know a little of the efficacy of a few medicines and after a while I found that a little knowl- edge of Cuticura was about all I needed. One of the very bad cases I had to deal with was that of a young woman who had come to us not only broken in spirit but in a most awful condition pnysically. Our doctor examined her and told us that she had scabies (the itch), incipient paresis, rheumatism, etc., brought on from exposure and the effects of her ragged-edged life. Her poor body was a mass of sores from scratching and she was not able to retain solid food. We tried many things, a good tonic was prescribed and baths with a rubbing of lard and sulphur. We worked hard for seven weeks and you can imagine how discouraged we were when, after all that time, we could see so little improvement. One day I hap- pened to see a Cuticura advertisement telling how a little baby had been cured of a bad case of skin eruption, and although I had but 97 cents (four shillings) with me, I bought a cake of Cuticura Soap and a bottle of Cuticura Resolvent. When I reached home I was like a child with a new toy, and we bathed our patient well and gave her a full dose of the Resolvent. She slept that night better than she had since she had been with us and the next day I located the price of a box of Cuticura Ointment. I am • not exaggerating when I say that in exactly five weeks this young woman was able to look for a position, being strong enough to work and full of ambition. In another month she left the home, strong and well. Laura Jane Bates, 85 Fifth Ave., New York, N. Y., U. S. A.. Mar. 11, '07." Sometimes Parliament does A MOMENTOUS entirely quiet, unosten- MEASURE, tatious, non-contentious work, which is barely noticed at the tirna, but may, nevertheless, be of greatest social and historic importance. To business of this unpretending kind the attention of the House of Commons was invited on Wednesday by the HOME SECRETARY. It may chance that the Bill introduced under the ten minutes' rule will,in the long course of time,and even before many years are over, commend itself to the people of the3e islands as one of the very finest and most beneficial measures ever passed by an English Parliament. Mr. GLADSTONE made no sort of fuss about it. He lavished no praise what- ever on his own Bill,and there was no opportunity for other members to express their opinion con- cerning it. Nevertheless we venture to regard it as a measure containing proposals which would make the present Government memorable if it did nothing else of which the historian need take note.

What is proposed in the new Crimes Bill is nothing short of a revolution in our present method of dealing with crime. We should have said, "in our present method of dealing with the criminal classes," if it were not the case that the Bill is intended to distinguish between crime and the habitual criminal. Mr. CTLADSTONE'S proposals mark the definite introduction of a new humane- ness of view into the attitude adopted by legisla- tion towards the perpetrators of crime. We have for many years been debating the question of whether the criminal law should be preventive or merely punitive. The Homn SECRRETARY'd Bill frankly takes the view that the prevention of crime is the chief object to be kept in view by the State. A. previous Government measure, the Children's Bill, proposes the abolition of imprisonment for children under fourteen years of age. The Bill introduced by the HOME SECRETARY yesterday is in a manner supplementary to this. OF its two great objects the first is to give to the punishment of all young criminals a reformatory character. It is a Ministerial adoption of the Borstal experiment. Borstal Prison, which stands on the hills above Ttochester, was a convict establishment until not many years since. It has more lately been a prison for boys and young man, where trades are taught, where the prisoners who are sent out are qualified to take up work in some honest trade, and in connection with which there is an Associa- tionyvhich undertakes to provide work for and to watch over every discharged prisoner. There are to-day Borstal departments in many of our prisons and they have already been the means of saving thousands of young men from a life of crime.

The first part of the HOME SECRETARY'S Bill adopts and extends the Borstal system. It is a great scheme of rescue for those who, at the close of their first term of imprisonment, would also certainly be driven into a life of crime if the re were not the Borstal system to save them. The HOME SECRETARY'S further proposal is to make a life of crime as nearly imp os Bible as it can be made. Sir ROBERT ANDERSON, thoughtful man, with a long and close experience of the prison population, proposed a few years since that recognised habitual criminals should be shut out from the possibility of committing crime. This pro- posal is, in a mild but effective form, embodied in the Bill of the HOME SECRETARY, The habitual prisoner behaves well in prison. He is rather fond of taking Communion. He knows how to avoid punishment, and when he is liberated from prison he always slips back to crime. The new Bill proposes that where a prisoner, convicted on indictment of a crime and sentenced to penal servitude, is found by the jury to be a habitual criminal, a further sentence can be passed that the prisoner be kept in detention on the termination

We Ar 3 presuming, of course, that it will become an Act of Parliament, for it must surely be endorsed by all men of 'commonsense and goodwill.

The success of the Waning Tug MunciL Muaical Festival leaves no FI3STIVAL. room fordount that interest in music and in _ its

advancement locally has made great headway of late years. The talent attracted was high and notwithstanding the out-door allure- touts of a delightful May Day, the public assembled in sufficiently large numbers to Ell the Parr Hall. All this must have been very encouraging to the promoters who had worked hard to ensure that Warrington should have every reason to be proud of its first festival. Whether the financial results will be completely satisfactory ?ealaine to be seen, for such an ambitious under- taking cannot be consummated without very heavy exPenditure. We hope, however, that the position Will be such as to justify the continuance of the festival as a permanent annual fixture. After the treat afforded on Saturday there should be no uncertainty as to the whole-hearted support of the townspeople. It was disappointing to see anch a 'small list of patrons of this year's festival but now that the objects of the movement have become more generally understood, generous financial aid Will probably be forthcoming for future enterprizas. The Committee were fortunate in enlisting the help of Mr,and Mrs. A.H. Crosfiald who presented tt valuable trophy for the chief competition. We , doubt if any better music has been heard in War- rington than the exquisite singing of the leading _ mixed choirs of the North who took part in this 1 aanlest—the gem of the festival. The local chapel , choirs also did themselves great credit and the Bold-street singers are to be complimented upon !vinning the cop presented by Mr. Dodds. The idea af crossing a hill THE NEW by means of a lake is not - Plakursta SCHEME. exactly new, but the sug- gestion for developing the Panama Canal upon these lines 851 t. above sea level is decidedly novel. The scheme is put for- Ward by exports on the spot as a solution of the entire difficulty. The turbulency after storm of t',,lle Ohagres River has always been a menace to c the work of the engineers, and the project now is - ;Int to provide adequate artificial outflow channels, but to ottoinduce the water to voread itself over an 220 square miles. With that once done, .-: the task of crossing the Isthmus will be .t.• comparatively simple. Ships are to be carried ,t oP to the lake by successive locks, and the i• structural key ,to the situation will be the ,teat Gluten dam, which will be a mile and a half Long at the top, and at the base half a mile thick, vessels, according to this scheme, will pass from .°oean to ocean in twelve hours, the distance being forty-nine miles. The locks on either side will be In three stages. This is the newest and most Picturesque of all the schemes, and expert opinions till'en it differ as greatly RS they have done upon I:ta predecessors. The tropical downpours of rain n,ave been an anxiety to engineers at all stages "ring works of construction, and the lake is both to c°lve this difficulty and to take ships from the Atlantic to the Pacific into the bargain.

THE POLITICS OF THE WEEK.

BY OUR LONDON CORIZESPOND2NT. Politics are now at their high tide. The events of this week and last, social and political, have been of striking importance and interest. As the result of some significant utterances of the Prime Minister last week, and of the stimulus of the Stirling election, the spirit, the enthusiasm, and the energy of the party in power are worked no to a high pitch. The important fiscal debate in the House of Lords, which concluded too late for notice last week, has also tended to con- centration of the issues in the political field. All politicians feel the truth of the Prime Minister's remark that the third year of a Parliament is the year of trial. We are now fairly in that critical period. FRENCH PRESIDENT AND GERMAN BURGOMASTERS.

The outstanding figures in the great scenes enacted this week in our midst are those of the King and the President of the French Republic. Their cordial meeting, and the signal marks of .honour paid to the foreign guest in the name of the nation, and with due State pomp and pageantry, have well satisfied, and at the same time strengthened, the sentiment of friend- ship and goodwill towards our nearest Continental neighbour. The Franco-British Exhibition, which the President came to see, has already often recalled the dreams of peace and goodwill between the nations of the world indulged in at the time of the Great Exhibition of 1851. Sometimes they have been recalled with cynical allusions to the contrast between those Alnaschar visions in a Crystal Palace then in Hyde Park, and the stern realities of the fifty at sixty years that have since passed. Bat the more prevalent and the happier sentiment may bo expressed in the sentiment" Let me dream again" those dreams of international amity, which after all tend, however slowly, to their own fulfilment. The near coincidence of the visit of the German Burgo-masters and their cordial reception by the King and by representative bodies, emphasised the comprehensiveness of our foreign sympathies, while those who are not happy unless they are asserting our own Imperial importance saw all this sentiment well- balanced by demonstrations of the Empire and Victoria Leagues: Sir Edward Grey was particularly felicitous in reconciling what to some minds are the somewhat opposing ideas conveyed by "Empire "and " Liberty." This year there has been less disposition to make Empire "a party asset, and Sir E. Grey's definition of Empire will live : " Liberty and independence, not subordination and restraint." THE KIN'S RUSSIAN VISIT.

Much interest is also taken in the King's contem- plated visit with the Queen to Russia ; but this, in some respects the most interesting manifestation of international friendship and sympathy, has produced a note of protest in Parliament by Mr. O'Grady, the Labour Member for East Leeds, on the ground of the political condition of that country and the persecution and imprisonment of the members of recalcitrant Dumas. _lf nations were to suspend all intercourse until they came on to each other's political or social standard, if one king could not visit another unless his responsible Ministers behaved themelves accord- ing to British notions of Ministerial propriety, there would be an end of ententes rordiales, and of all hopes of what we may call the " mutual improvement " of nations. The Prime Minister was most emphatic in his deprecation of these public reflections on the internal affairs of other countries in connection with royal visits ; and also reminded his interrogater of the relationship between the King and Queen and the Czar and Czarina, which makes the visit to Russia all the more natural.

===== 0002601_19080530_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
SIIRLING'S NEW MEHBER.

------- - When the House of Commons met on Monday, the new Member for Stirling took his seat as the fourth member quite recently sent up from Scotland to mark the impotency of brewer, Tariff Reformer, or bell- ringing suffragette, to shake the Government there. This succession of Liberal victories is alrthe more significant at a time when the House of Lords is claiming *to know the will of the Scottish- people regarding the measures proposed for their own country better than the elected House. Stirling sent Mr. Ponsonby by a greater majority than it had ever given at a contested election to Henry Campbell Bannerman himself; although, for days before the poll, it was being spread abroad that in the late Prime Minister's constituency there was -a reaction, and that the seat had only been held by a personal tie. The personal tie is maintained by the election of the Secretary of the previous member, and there are votes enough thrown into that pile of 1,360 over and above a bare majority to assure the new member that he is well tied politically too. BUDGETS AND TARIFF REFORM.

- - - The Budget Resolutions were finally passed 'early this week, and by Tuesday night the Finance Bill, which is to embody them in the Statute law, was intro- duced. The new Chancellor of the Exchequer enjoyed himself immensely on Monday night in defending the Budget proposals against the onslaughts of Ur. Austen Chamberlain and Mr., Boner Law, whose anxiety as so the provision for next year's Old Age Pensions (wholly on the Chancellor of the Exchequer's account!) did' not seem to trouble that buoyant statesman very much. Enough, he said, for the year was the finance thereof. One thing is certain : Mr. Lloyd-George is not going to look for his next year's money to new fiscal systems. The Lords' Debate on the Tariff Reform and Colonial Preference has much cleared the air on that subject. Lord Curzon showed a disposition to range himself with the Tariff Reformers, and has since done so more definitely ; but Lord St..A.ldwyn, while anxious to please his own party and dissatisfied with the Government's reduction of indirect taxation, was altogether unable to take up the Balfoar-cuna- Chaplin-cum-Chamberlain Tariff gospel. His frank explanation of his difficulties was more damaging to the Tariff Reformeause than an out-and out opponent's attack. That we should have a tariff, 90 as to be able to bargain with other countries who impose heavy duties on our manufactures, and also to give a prefer- ence to the Colonies, are the two main recommenda- tions of the fiscal "reformers." But these two recom- mendations, Lord St. Aldwyn painted out, will not work together. OUT-AND-OUT FREE TRADE PEERS.

- - - But the real kernel of the Fiscal Reform nut was quickly got at by the strong body of Free Trade Peers in the House of Lords. They don't find this at all a hard nut to crack. Lord Cromer, Lord Avebnry, Lord Wolverhampton, (still better known by his familiar name, Sir Henry Fowler), and Lord Crewe all dis- played the kernel as pure Protectioa. Lord Avebury quoted with great effect Sir William Lyne's illuininat- ing remark: "Importers are parasites on the trade of the Commonwealth." No British manufacturers are therefore wanted to compete with the Australian ones. The market is to be closed to us, and the " Preference " means that ours is to remain open to the Colonist, while we are good enough to close it to all the rest of the world just enough to give him a better chance. Imports and exports (it was pointed out) pay for each other, and the profit is to both aides By taking in our products Canada obliges us to take back more of hers in return cargoes. She develops her own boundless resources, and employs her people on the land, instead of driving them into towns to manufacture more than she can naturally, cheaply,and profitably do. The fact that a duty is required proves the unprofitableness of the indugry. The root of. the fiscal question is the understanding of free exchange, and of the fact that goods are not really paid for with money, which is but a counter for the goods, exchanged. LORD CROMER'S METLIOD. Lord Cromer succeeded in making the fiscal ques- tion humorous, and therefore interesting. He made fun of the scientific tariffs. What British merchant accustomed to Free Trade could tolerate their distinc- tions ; the salt sacks paying one duty, the salt bags another, the untaxed pen and the carefully taxed pencil? This is the method of investigation of concrete

cases, already recommended in this correspondence, as the touchstone of Tariff Reform. L3rd Cromer, however, also went back to the times when we had a system of Colonial preference before, with ludicrous results. To evade the duty our Brazil coffaa came round by the Cage, and wood from Norway and Sweden came to England through Canada ! There were among traders endless complaints and vexations. SHORTENED TALK AND DAYLIGHT SAVING.

Among popular by-topics of the day may be briefly mentioned the cry for shorter speeches in Parliament and the Daylight Saving Bill. The new Prime Minister sets a splendid example of brevity and lucidity in Parliamentary speech ; and some sort of moral pressure is wanted to check the loquacity of members where such good examples fail. Other remedies are alien to the very idea of Parliament, the citadel of free speech. When the Daylight Saving Bill got its second reading without any objection being taken, it was looked upon as a huge joke, and passed its stage with a good-humoured ironical cheer. In Committee it has received an astonishing amount of support ; and it is rapidly becoming a question of practical politics whether it is not possible by a little strategic clock-shifting—twenty minutes for each of four Sundays in April —to cheat us all into the habit of beginning our national and business day an hoar and twenty minutes earlier in the summer months,and so living more in sunshine and working less by artifical light. The practicability of the reform will for some time be severely debated ; but never has an idea originally received as a joke, and denounced in some quarters as a " wild-cat scheme," been so rapidly transformed into a question of genuine debate.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

A mother was pleading for her 8311 at St. Helens Police Court on Tuesday : " I hope your worships will be liniment with him, ' she said.

* * * At 5.27 p.m. yesterday the" buzzers" at Cockhedge blew a fearful and prolonged blast. " Fire " was immediately presumed, and, the helter-skelter rush from this neighbourhood towards" the factory" was startling. The news soon transpired, however, that the blast was merely a trial, and excitement quickly evaporated.

* * * An important public improvement was carried out on Thursday, i.e. the re-paving of the tramway track on the east side of Warrington bridge. It has been in a scandalous state of disrepair for a year or more, and the wonder has been that no serious accidents could be traced to the holes in the pavement. We had almost despaired of seeing an improvement until we got a new bridge, but perhaps the fact that so many dignitaries were due at Latchford on Thursday after- noon aroused the Paving and Sewerage Committee to a sense of their shortcomings. • • The library which Mr. Andrew Carnegie opened at Darwen on Wednesday brings the number of libraries he has presented to towns in different parts of the world to over 1,800. Even Mr. Carnegie's memory has its lapses when dealing with the details of•hat vast total. Not long ago, it was told at Wednesday's meeting, he was walking in Chester with a friend, when a man came up to him and effusively thanked him for giving the library to Knutsford. "1 am glad," Mr. Carnegie repliee, that you find it useful." Ile shook hands with his admirer, who went on his way. Then Mr. Carnegie, turning to his friend, inquired, " And where is Knutsford ?" * ,k *

As we were lunching on the side of Kinder Scout on Tuesday (writes a " Manchester Guardians" cor- respondent) we noticed a solitary lady pedestrian coming up the Kock from Hayfield. She strode out manfully, but paused, curiously enough, whenever she came to a projecting rock. When, half an hour later, we followed on the same route, we found chalked on every rock we passed the words " Votes for Women." The artist of our party became an anti-suffragist on the spot. The rest of us gloomily speculated on the future state of English landscapes if thess methods of propaganda should spread and we should find, perhaps, " Your beer will cost you more" flaunting itself on Helvellyn. •* * * Apropos of the Daylight Bill now before Parliament . .

it vill be interesting to note that a movement, has been started in one of the larg)st engineering offices in town to utilise the light hours of the day for open-air enjoyment. Now that the fine weather appears to have really come, everything points to the venture being an unqualified success. The first out- ing was a cycle trip to Houghton Green on Friday starting at G a.m , and a very enjoyable game of bowls was participated in. We think this movement might be copied with advantage by many other offices in town, and to those who have perforce to spend the greater partion of each day in an office the beneficial effect of these matutinal spins is obvious.

* * * The Transvaal mines output of gold for April, 190, was £2,403,503. This is slightly less than that of the preceding month, but April had one less working day than March, and its daily average, as a Tory paper's City editor points out, still "constitutes a record." Such dividends as the following, paid in 1907, enforce the lesson :

Crown Deep Ferreira Deep 3nsl PP . 12 Geldenhuis Deep Nouse Mines 25 „ „ , Rose Deep The chairman of some of these companies prophesied higher dividends in the near future. That is how a Liberal Government " ruins " the Rand.—" Morning Leader." _ .

The Assessment Cammatee created somewhat of a sensation yesterday by proposing to raise several local works assessments to an unprecedented figure. The Firth Company were to be lifted from £317 to £B2l ; Messrs. Pearson and Knowlee from £4,62.0 to 17,207 ' • and Messrs. Rylands Bros, Ltd. from £3,470 to £7,250. Naturally the secretaries of these Com- panies all protested and were granted an adjournment in order that the valuer and representatives of the firms might confer. We hope when the Committee has settled with the manufacturers that it will proceed to take its full pound of flesh from the breweries and licensed houses. It is openly asserted that there is plenty of under-assessment here which requires remedying.

* * * One of the late Tom Morris's dicta had reference to Sunday golf. "If you two gentlemen dinna need a rest on the Sabbath," he once said to two English- men, "the links does." When Mr. Carnegie was in St. Andrews six years ago, receiving the freedom of the city, he had a long chat with the veteran, closing the conversatiOn by handing him his card, on which he wrote "Toni Morris, King of Golfdotn, your loyal subject, A..e." - Fervently patriotic, Old Toni" was always to the fore in national celebrations. Dr. Tulloch mentions that he helped to carry the Royal and Ancient's famous silver clubs and bills in the Coronation procession through the streets of St. Andrews ; while the day arter Lord Roberts entered Pretoria, an event synchronising with the start of the Open Championship, Tom Morris was down at the first tee sporting a aid, white, and blue rosette. * * * Addressing the Lyn= Primrose . Leaguers on Monday, Mr. W. Peter Rylands instanced Germany as a pattern empire. Said he: Germany they never saw anybody who seemed poor and out of work. Everybody was well clothed and well fed, and they never saw the distress which was rampant in this country. The country bore an air of extreme prosperity." Accepting Mr. Rylands' rosy view we should conclude that Germany was an El Dorado. But facts and figures do not bear out this. According to official figures the number of persons in Prussia with an income of over 17s. 3d. per week (Z 45 a year) is 3,895,181 out of a population of about 36,000,000. In Britain there are a million people with over £l5O a year, and in Prussia only 460,000. If there is no poverty or unemployment in Germany, the people must have solved the problem of living on air. * *

We usually find something amusing in the reports of Primrose League meetings. There is such a 1 grandiloquent, take-care-of-the-world air 'about thee gatherings. At Lynam on Monday there was much pompous talk about Imperialism. -Ur. W. P. Rylands discoursed about empires that have been, and the one that is —thanks to the Primrose League. The gem of his speech as repotted in our Wednesday contem- porary is as follows : " There could be no doubt the present Emperor of Germany was imbued with the imperial idea. The Emperor and Bismarck, one of the greatest statesmen of the past century, had always acted along the line he (Mr. Ilylands) had suggested, viz., the policy of extending the Empire into the farthest districts of the world." (Applause.) We had thoughts of cabling the German Emperor to ask if this was so, but we hardly like to doubt Mr. itylands' word

Saving Daylight. Mr. J. J. Crosfield, depuV chairman and managing director of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Limited, Warrington, gave evidence before the Select Com- mittee of the House of Commons on the Daylight- !saving Bill on Thursday. He said that the company employed 1,593 men. He believed that the Bill would be of the greatest possible value to w irking men generally, and he joined issle with Sir George Livessy, chairman of the Metropolitan Gas Company, in this respect. In June, 1931, his firm altered the hours of their Office staff from 9 and 5.30 to 8.39 and 5, the intention being to revert to the old hours in October. But when October came the staff, which numbered 103, asked to b 3 allowed to continue the summer hours. The hours in the fsctory were from 7.30 to 5.33. The difficulty of m skin a change there was that the now dinner hour would not as a rule fit 11 with the general household arrangements of the workers.

WATER FIRENTORKS.—See the Fairy Fountain at the Trades Exhibition, Parr Hati.—Advt. PUBLIC BENEVIT.—UNDER THI CLOCK TOR BEST BOOTS IN LAIWASIIIRE.—Advt. • Serious Leather Thefts. At Warrington, on Viredaesday, Charles Steele, Aiken-street ' • John Magian, Erwood-street ; George Ellis, Elizabeth-street ; awl Frederick Bloomfield, Goulden-street ; four youths, were charged with breaking and entering the works of Randall Bros., Limited, Tanner's-lane, on two dates in April and May, and stealing fourteen bends of leather, of the value of £l2 10i.—A1r. A. Browne, who prosecuted, said a remand would be necessary. in order that the Chief Constable might make inquiries as ta where the leather had been disposed of. The prisoners had admitted the offence, and it appeared that a portion of the stolen property had been sold to local shoemakers. In consequence of a communication Detective Woolla- cott secreted himself in the shop of Mr. Clarke, Liverpool-road. He there heard prisoner Bloomfield offer a portion of the leather for sale. This lei to his arrest, and subsequently the other three youths were also arrested. The leather was in an unfinished state, and ought not to have been bought by anyone who understood the trade.—The magistrates granted a remand until Tuesday next. DON'T Fortorr.—High-cliss vocalists at the Trades Exhibition, Parr Hall.--Advt. Death of Mr. W. Bradbury. -

We regret to announce the death of a well-known Warrington resident, Mr. William Bradbury, of 327, Lovely-lane, on the 21st inst. after a comparatively short illness. Theceased was employed at the Pearson and Knowles works as a shingler for over 28 years. He came to Warrington 38 years ago and commenced work at the Whitecross Works but after working there for about 10 years he joined the Pearson and Knowles Co., by whom he was employed up to the last. He was a Liberal of the old school and took an active 'part in the last election. He was very generally respected and his loss will be keenly felt by a large circle of frien4lle leaves behind Itirn a widow and eight children to mourn his loss. The funeral took place on Monday last at the Warrington Cenetery, the chief mourners being his three sons, John, Joe and Bart. Toe firm were represented by Mr. Rowe, the bearers being ten of his old fellow workmen. A. number of beautiful wreaths were sent by the widow, John Bradbury and family, Joseph Bradbury and family, Bert Bradbury and family, Mrs Appleton, Rebecca (daughters) Nellv and Ada (grandchildren), \f r. and Mrs. Scott, Mr. and Mrs. James, junior, Mr. and Mrs. Kennon, Mr. and Mrs. James, senior. KING Corrox will open the Trades Eshibition, Parr Hall, on Tuesday.—Advt. Reduction in Sugar. AsgrAvv's,4,Buttermarket st.—Ad v t Legh-street Tea and Concert.

The quarterly tea and concert ia connection with Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church was held on Thursday evening, when a large number sat down to tea, after which a very successful concert was pro- ceeded with, under Coo presidency of Miss A. Rowlands, who gave a brief address. The concert opened with a a pianoforte duet by the Misses Titchett, which was executed in fine 'style. Then followed a sneezing chorus by the young ladies, entitled "The merry month of May," and whieh humor- ously iltustrated the results which accrue from putting on summer clothing too early in the season. Miss May Lord, of Manchester, who has a fine contralto voice of pleasing quality 'and good range,was well received in her songs " The Toilers" and" Sing me to 'sleep " for the latter of which she ! was encored. Miss A. Woodland was happy in her selection of " What's a little maid to do?" and the piquancy that,this song called for was cleverly dis- played, and delighted the audience. Miss A. Bridge rendered the fine song " Oh Beating waves," and her delicate treatment of same revealed true artistic taste. The song and chorus " There was a mouse," by Miss J, Shaw and choir was well received as were also two humorous recitations by Miss Buglass, " Hanging a Picture "and "The Inventor's Wife" the latter ,taking well with the audience. The instrumental duet: " Men of Harleclo " and variations pldy,ed oa toy instruments by Misses Bridge and Buglass was very amasing and an encore wasdemanded. A musical sketch next followed which consisted of instrumental selec- tions by the young ladies who had a full band of tpy instruments. Song, " Love's old sweet song" by Miss Cotten ; song, The Happy Jappy " and tambourine Solo by Miss Elwell ; ventriloquial sketch by Miss L Buglass ; chorus, " Schnider's Band," Miss A. Bridge taking the solo (encored). The last item was a selec- tion by band" Swannie River," and concluded a pro- gramme which for fun and variety left little to be , desired. The fall list of performers was as follows: I —Miss %glass, Miss L. Buglass, Misses A. Bridge, Cotton, Elwell, Parkes, J. Shaw, A. Shaw, Spruce, Titchett, A. Woodland, N. Woodland and Lord. Miss A.. GARLAND, 51-55, Bridge street, Warrington.—Advt. „Warrington Shop Theft. John Grant, a labourer, of Naylor-street, pleaded guilty at the Warrington Police Court, on Thursday, Ito stealing 2s. 31. in coppers from the shop of Robert Emmett,pork butcher,Buttermarket-street. On Wednes- day night prosecutor left the shop for a few minutes to go,into the back kitchen. Rearing a noise, he hurried back and saw prisoner at the back of the counter with the drawer open and some coppers in his hand, which ho placed in his pocket. After a struggle to get away prisoner was given into the custody of Police-constable Bennett. The magistrates committed him for three months.
