HEALTH OF THE TOWN. Medical Officar's Annual Rapp/.

The first annual report of Dr. J. Coote Hibbert, Medical °Meer of Health for Warrington, has been issued this week, and forms a comprehensive volume of over 60 pages. In the coufse of his report, Dr. Hibbert says, after paying a compliment to the work of the late Dr. Gornall end Dr. Gaffikin, the general, the zymotic, and the infantile death-rates all show a marked improve- ment on those for the previous year. Thus the improve- ment in this respect, which is evident in most of the large towns of England nd Wales, has also shown itself in Warrington, and it will be extremely gratifying to the Committee to find that durina the year in which their actions have been much criticised,the chief mortality rates have been the lowest on record. How far the repeated flushing of the town with rain and the accompanying cool weather during the greater part of the summer are responsible for these results, the vtatistics for a warmer and drier summer will show, but at any rate we have here a hint that the thorough cleansing of streets and especially of courts and back passiges roust be scrupulously carried out. And I would lay down this proposition that,other conditions being constant, the death-rates for any town —and this especially applies to the zymotic and infantile rates—will be high or low according as animal or :vegetable refuse is allowed to accumulate in its midst or the converse. Nowhere is organic matter a greater danger than in enclosed courts and ill-c4ntilated b3.6k-yards, and very special attention should be paid to the cleansine, of and the removal of all refuse from these areas, and as far as possible water-closets should be substituted fox pail-closets in these parts of the Borough. At present many dwellings receive their so-called fresh air more or less directly from the atmosphere of the pail- closet, which more often than not in the poorer districts is surrounded by liquid filth which has in some way escaped the pail. The question of convefting the present pail-closet into a water-closet system, and of re-sewering the town is, lam quite aware, a very dull- cult one and is certainly too large a subject for me to bring forward at the present time ; but so long as the present system remains, the sanitation of Warrington cannot be said to be satisfactory. That the majority of houses should have a collection of human excreta hard by their beck doors and pervading the air of their very larders is surely a blot on present-day sanitation. The report then deals at length with the chief causes of death, tuberculosis claimiog the largest number of victims (139), or one-eighth of the total number of deaths from all causes. The milk an'l meat supplies are discussed carefully. Paragraphs also deal with the prevalence of scarlet fever, diphtheria, and measles, and a special chapter is devoted to Infantile Mortality. The most notable feature is a reduction in the number of deaths from diarrhcea-100 in 1906, and only 32 in 1907. Slaughter-houses and the need for a public abbatoir are also commented upon. A section is devoted to the work of female inspector. 'TOWN COUNCIL MEETING. ' 'The report will be discussed at the ordinary meeting Li of the Town Council to be held on Tuesday evening.

THE BOLTON SCHOOL. OPENING ITETIEMONY.

- - .

Early Latchford Reminiscences.

Warrington's second Council School—the Bolton School at Latchford—was formally opened on Tnursday afternoon, in the presence of a large and representative gathering, by Councillor J. Fulton Smith (chairman of the Education Committee). Ideal weather favoured . the ceremony. The school has been erected from the designs of aressre. J. E. Wright, Geroett and Wright, architects, .tc., Warrington. by Mr. Harry Fairclough, and it is commodious, well lighted, and handsomely equipped. There are two departments—the infants' on the ground floor and the girls' on the first floor. Each department is arranged for 330 scholars and is divided into seven class rooms. A valuable adjunct is the large play-ground, part of which has been laid out as gardens for nature study. The school will be open on Monday next and the infants and girls now attending the Grappentiall-road Council School end Christ Chnrck School respectively will be transferred to it. The head mistresses are Miss Place (senior department) and Miss Beardmore (infants). Four hundred and twenty scholars were drawn up in the school yard on Thursday afternoon and greeted the visitors with hearty cheers. The Mayoress (Mrs. Smethurst) and Mrs. Fulton Smith were presented with beautiful bouquets by Minnie Wells and Bertha Muskett respectively. The opening ceremony took place in the central hall of the girls' department and was presided over by Alderman Pierpoint. Amongst others present were : The Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman end Mrs. Smethurst),Councillor and Mrs. Fulton Smith,Mr.Henry hoberts (vice-chairman of the committee), Aldermen Bennett, Evans, Ellison, Revs. Canon Stevenson, G. C. Briggs, J. F. Matthews, Fathers Wilson, Whittle. Bugling, Marrs and Roche, Councillors Wilson, Barton, Skelland, Steel, Dickenson, Pemberton, Broadhurst and Ball, Dr. Hibbert, Colonel J. D. Buckton. Messrs. C. Mawson, A. T. Hallway, Stanley Wright, IL Garnett, R. T. Fairclough. C. Jackson Holmes, W. A. Proud, Reuben Higham. H. Hallard, E. Place, W. Gill, E. 8. A. Robson, F. J. Ruddock, W. Mason, A. J. Jolley, John White, C. S. Burgess, T. P. Bradshaw, Harry Fairclough, H. Ringdon, John Fairclough, John Fairhurst, 'W. Bibby, A. M. Nee, W. Miles, J. Moore Murray (secretary and director), R. B. Roberts (assistant), and manyladies. The CHAIRMAN, in an interesting address, spoke of the changes which had been effected in Latchford as compared with the time when he entered it, almost as a schoolboy, in the year 1855. At that time Latchford was one of the prettiest villages in Cheshire ; certainly it was rich in its picturesqueness and in its botanical specimens. It was difficult to imagine Wash-lane as it appeared its his youthful days. It was then, he believed, one of the choicest old lanes in Cheshire. and was replete from one end to the other with sixteen or eighteen well-known varieties of ferns. To-day he was afraid they would not find a single one. At that time, if he recollected aright, Latchford had no educational facilities of its own. The children were compelled to en to Grappenhall school, end it was only a little before IS6O that the Christ Church school was opened, and a very small school it was. It partook more of the nature of a dame school presided over by a lady of most beautiful character who was deeply attached to the scholars, and they were deeply attached to her. There were no schools at the other end of the township, and it was a curious fact that the people who lived on the Latchford side of the Black Bear Bridge considered they had no connection with the people who lived on the other side. (Laughter) They were looked upon as two separate and distinct communities, thinking out different ideas and ideals. The population was composed mostly of egricniturists, tanner's labourers and boatmen. Now all that had been changed—he did not know whether it, had bean for the worse. He regretted very nnich that the old picturesqueness bad passed away but he supposed the old order must give way to the new. At the laying of the memorial stone of the Beamont School he made a few remarks which he believed at the time were received with anything but the spirit in which he intended them and caused some little disturbance of spirit on the part of some members of the Education Committee. He felt, however, that he was luetifled in saying what he did. He then intimated that in all matters of educational and eepeliellv in the building of schools, while he would not spare any money at all in promoting the building iif schools as far as the comfort and health of the teaching staff and pupils were concerned, he did object to the wasting of money upon Unnecessary ornaments. HO believed the effect of those remarks was that the cost of the Bolton School was cut down in accordance with what he said or with the tendency of the spirit of the time. As far as he was able to judge now, he could not find a single fault with it. He thought the school was perfectsand if the elevation of the outside was not qeite al artistic and ornamental as some of their educational friends would like, he was still of the opinion that it fulfilled all the wants of the schools, and he hoped they would go on building schools of a similar character. That school was called after an ex-Mayor, who, he was sorry, was not there that day, because one would have thought that the matter would have been of sufficient importance to him when his name was going to be perpetuated for time immemorial. At all events he thought Alderman Bolton might have given up some of those pleasures of his at the Temple Show in London, and been with them that day. 'I hey would all have been very pleased to see him, and they regretted his absence very much. He (Alderman Pier- point) did not agree with some of the actions of Alder- man Bolton, but he did credit him with being actuated with ,a spirit which had for its object the betterment of the condition of the people and the improvement of the town. Alderman Pierpoint expressed his satisfac- tion that on the contract for the schools the extras were a minus quantity; and he thought that reflected great credit on the architects and builder. Some people thought the Education Committee were going ahead a little too fast, but he did not. They could not spend too much on education so long as they spent it wisely and well. He hoped the Committee would as early as possible consider the desirability of erecting a new boys' school fer that neighbourhood. (Applause.) Councillor FULTON 'SMITH then delivered an address in which he urged the importance of sympathy and good feeling between the teacher and the scholar. Unless there was an unwritten understanding between the two, there would be dulness and failure. The competition of the world grew keener every day and it behoved them to look into the education question nationally and to see that they were bringing up their children to cope with the competition which seemed to enter into every part of social life. Unless we were well equipped we should fall behind in the race and the prestige, honour and glory of this Empire might be impaired. The aim of the teacher was to bring out what the children knew and the Board of Education started a system of examinations with that object,. The idea vras very noble in its way bnt iteresulted in the teachers cramming the children so that they might pass the examination. We were livlug in better times now, and the examinations did not take that hard and fast line but the inspectors tried to see if the children understood what they had been taught. Unlesm there was the solid founda- tion of the troths of the Bible no teaching would be of any use to the children in their social life and in the hereafter. That could not be done by simple lessons. There must be the influence of the character and con- duct of the teacher, that noble ideal of truth and righteousness, so that the children when they entered the battle of life would know the difference between right and wrong and their leanings would be towards the right. He trusted that there would be many years of successful teaching within those walls and that the children would come there as if they were coming to some grand gala every day, feeling that the school was their home, and that they would look back upon their school days as being the happiest period of their lives. (Applause). Councillor ELLISON, In moving a vote oftthanks to Councillor Fulton Smith, said he could remember the time when they had no school or place of worship in that part of Latchford. The first provision made was the erection of the Latchford infants' school in 1844, at the opening of which he was present, three years before the incorporation of the borough. In 1860 the Baptist Church was built. That was followed by the erection of Christ Church, the Temperance Hall, the Parish • Room, the Wesleyan Church, and last year the Wesleyan Sunday school, at the opening of all of which, with the exception of the Parish Room, he wss present. He took a leading part in the establishment of the services at Christ Church. He served there under three Vicars, with all of whom he got on very well, especially con- sidering that he was a Methodist. (Laughter.) There was plenty of work yet to be accomplished in that part of Latchford and he wished success to all educatonal efforts. (Applause). Mr. R. T. Faincaoucri in seconding said he lived in a parish—Latchford Without—where to-day they had neither el:larch, chapel, school, nor public-house. (Laughter.) If they had to hold a meeting they had to go out of the parish. s The MAYOR supported and said he believed that school would compare favourably with any in Lanca- shire. (Applause.) Mr. IL ROBERTS also supported and urged the importance of thinking imperially, and not locally or parochially, on the question of education. Money well spent upon education .was never wasted but would be repaid a hundred fold an the time that was to come. , A vote of thanks was passed to the Chairman on the !proposition of Mr. W. Bunny seconded by Councillor WrasoN.

At the invitation of Councillor Fulton Smith. the visitors then partook of afternoon tea in the infants'

In the evening the school was open to the inspection of the public and many availed themselves of the privilege.

REMIND YOURSELF.

When you order in your stores of bread and cakes from your baker and confectioner,you should remember that the more plentiful the currants, the better will be the cake or the bun.

Currants are simply all nutriment, and are now used freely as a health-food. They build up the body, cleanse and enrich the blood, impart energy and vitality to the brain, and are everywhere recognised as one of the most sustaining foods it is possible to procure.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS

The Auditor and the Contract System.

A meeting of the Warrin,gton Board of Guardians was W held at the Workhouse on ednesday under the presi- dency of the Rev. T. R. Matthews. There were also present :—Miss Broadbent, Mrs. Simpson, Mrs. Wilkie- son, Mrs. Collingwood, Rev. M. Ryan Messrs. G. Mather, J. Evans, W. Gill, W. ,C. Brooks, W. Furness, R. Bolton, A. Brooks, W. Furness, W. Houghton J. Cook, P. Longton M. W. Seton, T. Ritchie' J. C. Sutton (clerk), E. 'J. Sutton (acting assistant clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A. Wright (superintendent of Padgate Schools).

THE PADGATE DHAPLAIN. A letter was received from the Bishop of Liverpool sanctioning the appointment of the Rev. G. W. Dickinson, Vicar of Padgate, as chaplain at the Padgate Industrial Schools at a salary of SW.

===== 0002601_19080530_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
PROPOSED BILLIIRD TABLE FOR OFFICERS.

At a meeting of the House -Committee on May 15th it was resolved to accede to the petition of the female officers that the guardians defray the cost of making up their uniforms; the matron to obtain quotations. At a meeting on May 22nd six tenders for the making up of uniforms, dresses and aprons were considered and two accepted. Miss Broadbent gave notice that the com- mittee consider the advisability of providing the male officers with a billiard table for their recreation room. The Roman Catholic Bishop of Liverpool (Dr. White- side) visited the institution and placed on record the fact that he was struck by the air of cheerfulness throughout and especially by the perfection of the new buildings. The minutes were approved. AUDITOR AND TUE CONTRACT SYSTEM.

The Local Government Board wrote asking for the Board's observations on the report of Mr. E. W. Hicks, district auditor' after his audit of the accounts of the Union for the half year ending at Michaelmas 1907. The auditor stated that he did not think the methods now obtaining in regard to the acceptance of tenders and the placing of contracts were satisfactory. At a previous audit he asked for the whole of the tenders to be produced and was informed by the Master that only the accepted ones were in his possession. He referred to the Clerk and gave him to understand that all tenders should be preserved as a means of comparison and that he should require their production in future. At the audit now under report the Workhouse Master again appeared and in reply to his request for the tenders produced a rough book of minutes of the House Committee in which the following resolution was pointed out :—" September 20th, 1'307. Upon a division it was resolved by five votes to four that the unsuccessful unopened tenders be destroyed by burning after the Board have confirmed the accepted contracts." It appeared, continued the auditor, that. the House Com- mittee minute book did not go before the Board but the Visiting Committee minute book which did was produced as the connecting link between the House Committee minute book and the minute book of the Guardians. After describing the method of opening tenders, the auditor said he was aware that the Guardians might by a resolution adopting any report made to them by n conimittee constitute the recom- mendation in such report an act of the Guardians, but the informality which he thought existed and which he begged leave to submit for the Board's consideration was that the opening of the signed tenders was made in committee end not before the full Board.

Mr. Huoolcs in moving that the report be referred to the House Committee said that technically there might be an informality but actually there was none. Tho House Committee consisted of twenty or twenty-one members—almost the whole of the Board—and they dealt with the tenders. If it were a committee of half- a-dozen he could quite understand that there would be reason for the complaint but at present he did not see any. Councillor JAMES EVANS seconded. _ .

„ The CLERK submitted that the real grievance of the auditor was that he could not have access to the unaccepted tenders whiith, were destroyed. Bitooxs : That is a trouble I should expect the House Committee to deal with. The committee 1 orrnu- lated the rules by which we now work and they did it in what they considered to be the best interests of ths Board and the contractor. The resolution was agreed to.

DOCK TO TAKE VESSELS A THOUSAND FEET LONG. Mr. Robert Gladstone, who was the guest of the Liverpool Chamber of Commerce ou Monday night, said in the Dock Board's new scheme provision was made for steamers a thousand feet long, and he should not be surprised—though he might not live to see it— if the day came when there would be steamers a thou- sand feet long running between Liverpool and New York.

FUBLIC V. BREWER.

LICENSING BILL MEETING

===== 0002601_19080530_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
AT WYCLIFFE,

MEETING AT IiVYCLIFFE CHURCH.

REV. JOHN YONGE AND THE ANGLICAN CLERGY'S ATTITUDE. Speeches by Mr. Ziba Armitage, Mr. H.

Roberts and others.

In the coarse of his sermon on the use and misuse of Empire Day at Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday evening, the Rev. Jolts YONGE said the nation had before its notice at the present time one of the greatest and boldest attempts at national, if not imperial, welfare which our age had ever witnessed. It was exciting the attention of all classes of the people to a prefounder degree, perhaps, than any measure which had ever previously been proposed. According to the inevitable necessities of Government by party, this Licensing measure had been introduced by the political party which was at present in power. That fact appeared to be enough to assure the party which was in opposition of the utterly iniquitous character of the proposals that were made. Sometimes the feel- ing arose in the mind that partizanship in politics was but of dubious virtue, and that it hindered the highest progress of the State, rather than helped it. But the measure of which he spoke was a so-called" Liberal " measur.?, and consequently the political prejudices of nearly or quite all of that assembly were in its favour. He wanted, however, to affirm with all emphasis he could, that they were not entitled to consider it that night as a matter of party politics. That church sad pulpit were not concerned with the interests of any political party, but they were , and ever would be interested in the welfare of the nation and the righteousness of it. He asked them to consider that measure, and if they would do so, to vote in its favour at the after meeting, not as a measure of Liberal politics, but of national temperance reform. He wanted them to understand that the special and supreme attention of all the Congregational Churches on this subject had been directly asked for by the leaders of their denomination, and all the Free Churches were being similarly called to take a conspicuous and pro- nounced part in this moral national battle for righteous- ness. He knew it would be said by some that these "political" movement should not be alloAed to intrude upon Sabbath hours and Sabbath services. But they who raised those objections appeared to him to be alto- gether blind to the greatness and urgency of the moral conflict to which the nation was now committed. They did not realise that it was infinitely more than a political controversy ; it was A GREAT MORAL RATTLE

that was now raging and ona which affected the moral life and health of the whole nation. Everybody knew perfectly well that the greatest national evil to-day was the evil of drunkenness. The nation had been in the deadly grip of that which called itself "the trade" for generations, and things now had come to such a pass that" the trade" was defying not only the State but the Church with its menaces, its threats and its most relentless hostility. Every public-house in Warrington was to-day a committee-room against the Licensing Bill. Its walls were plastered with inflammatory and misleading and fallacious placards, denouncing the Bill and stirring the people to resist and defeat it. It was therefore necessary that every congregation of Christian , people should become active and outspoken in favour of the greatest Parliamentary attempt ever yet made to gain supreme control of that übiquitous trade which had done so much injury to the health aud morality of the nation. If they did not espouse this measure and defeat the trade, the trade would ride roughshod over them. Mr. Yong.e then recapitulated the main pro- visions of the Bill. He maintained that if they diminished sources of drink they would diminish the drinking. Diminish the drinking, and they diminished the drunkenness. Diminish the drunkenness, and they diminished the consequent wretchedness, misery, vice, shame and crime. Reduce the number or temptations, and they reduced the number of those who fell. Reduce the number of those who fell and thoy increased the number of lives uplifted, of homes cleansed and improved, of families saved from degradation, of wives, mothers and children as well as husbands and fathers, raised to integrity, comfort and peace. The trade was furious, of course. It talked of confiscation, robbery,plunder,wanton sacrifice of widows and orphans and so on. And yet when the Royal Commission over which in 199 D Lord Peel presided, gave its report, it was signed among others by no less than eight of the principal brewers and this was their testimony It. is undeniable that a gigantic evil remains to be remedied, and hardly any sacrifice would be too great which would result in a marked diminution of this national degrada- tion. Nor is Parliament likely to rest satisfied with leaving things as they are." He thought they should do at least some of the great brewers the justice to believe that in their hearts they regretted the degrada- tion which their trade inflicted upon such multitudes of their fellow men. What did, however, prove to be to himself an

INSOLUBLE PROBLEM was that so many persons who themselves led estimable lives and so often engaged in philanthropic enterprises, could be content to grow wealthy and to live in luxury and affluence, knowing that their trade was the unquestionable source of the greatest part of social misery in all classes of the notion. He said it with all respect : he could not understand those persons. He could not understand the Christian, the disciple of Jesus Christ, who got his wealth in that way. He did not condemn : he simply could not understand. But he did understand this, and hoped the overwhelming majority of them did, that it had become the paramount duty of the Churches of Christ to do all in their power to carry this great Government measure to success. Surely it must be with all who cared for the highest welfare of the nation, a matter for great gladness that the furtherance of the measure was not being left exclusively to Noncon- formist?. The action that was being taken by

===== 0002601_19080530_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE MAJORITY OF THE LEADERS 01' THE ANGLICAN

CHURCH

Was most noble and WOitbi:- It was to all of those who were present a great inspiration and encouragement when the Bishop of Liverpool made his noble pronounce- merit in the Parr Hall. It was an act on his part of praiseworthy courage and of fine Christian fidelity. The regret was both sincere and deep and universal that the Bishop had to stand there unsupported by his clergy. He (the preacher) could not but believe their unanimous absence on that occasion had been regretted by not a few of them since. Already it was being stated that opinions among them were becoming more favourable. Bow well it would be if they would boldly say so ; if they would so help and encourage many of their con- gregations who were grieved at their apparent favour of the enemies of this great national reform. What a fine thing would it be in this brewery town, if Parr Hall could witness a combined gathering, such as was seen in the Albert Hall, in London on Saturday week ; if they could listen once again to the Bishop of Liver- pool,backed now by his clerical brethren,speaking out as he did before, with all the added inspiration of the rallied forces of his Church. Whatever might be the attitude which the Anglican Church in this town chose to maintain in this great moral conflict, he (Mr. Yonge) trusted that the Free Churches would, everyone of them, speak out with clear, decisive voice in favour of this measure. and that they at Wycliffe would he found solid and nnited in is promotion. For it aimed at a great national reform—a reform of the life and habits of the people, a reform in the homes, an uplift from the degradation which disgraced the Empire at its centre That would be a glad and glorious Empire Day when the diminished opportunities for vice and sin should produce a purer and sweeter, a stronger and nobler national life. It was their duty to pray for it and to speak for it, and to work for it, and to persevere in agitation until they achieved success. Until this be realized and the people of these realms be raised to purer and better life, Empire Day could never become a cloudless day, but must ever remain a day of many shit dows,regrets and disappointments.So long as drunken- ness remained the national sin, it could never be said that righteousness exalteth our nation. At the conclusion of the service a public meeting in support of the Licensing Bill was held, the Rev. John Yonge presiding over a good attendance.

===== 0002601_19080530_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
-THE RESOLUTION.

Mr. ZIII \ ARM ;TAG g moved the following resolution: —" That this meeting, held after divine service in Wycliffe Congregational Church, Warrington, on the evening of Empire Dey, Sunday, May 24th, 1908, rejoices greatly in the passing of the second reading in the House of Commons of the Government Licensing Bill by a majority of 21G, and earnestly trusts that with such amendments as may be deemed judicious for the strengthening of its proposals it may speedily becom3 law." Host of them present, he said, would only with great reluctance take part in such a meeting ' as that under present conditions of time and place if the issues at stake were wholly or chiefly political. But considering the matter in question, as they were bound to do, from the religious and national points of view, they said that here was a case of the moral wel- fare of the people against the financial interests of the trade ; and it was quite clear that they were acting consistently in supporting a measure which promised to promote more healthy conditions of national life. Christian people who realised the dreadful evils associ- ated with the driuk traffic, ft 9 now conducted, could not refuse to exert all the influence they possessed in order to strengthen the hands of those who sought to control

that incubus in the interests of nitional righteousness and well-being. The claims of property and privilege were sustained in great force by large numbers of t e wealthy classes, and they never lacked noisy and less I reputable advocacy. The

FORCES OF REACTION AND SELF-INTEREST were unifer.nly ranged against proposals designed for the welfare of the masses of the people, and there had been during the past few months many evidences of the discreditable length to which some supporters of so-called vested interests would go in resisting that great measure which they had come there to support. In the natur,l of the case the enemies of licence reform found ready to hand unlimited opportunities for influ- encing private opinion. Every licensed public-house in the country was a place for discussion, and too often for misrepresentation and intimidation, and emissaries of the trade came there face to face with victims, who were not always in a condition to exercise their reason- ing faculties. He was not saying that no persons of r repute and no great and good men had taken their stand ' against the Licensing Bill, though perhaps chiefly on points of detail. It was% too true that many men, of whom they hoped better things,had followed the call of the trade in resisting adeqa ite control of the business proved to be responsible for an over-whelming share of the prevailing poverty, lunacy, crime and misery which atiticted the body politic ; and yet how much more serious was it that the trade had been able to command support, even from some teachers of the religion of Jesus Christ. Obviously they were united in

ALLtaNcE-or. ritsasTnous rowrzxr with all the forces of evil. In face of an opposition of unexampled bitterness, the fight for this Bill was critical and strenuous, and they were warned by those who knew that unless the churches and religious societies manifested a full and hearty approval and took their uncompromising stand on the side of the Bill, th 3 Government would fad it almost impossible to carry it into law without making destructive con- cessions to the Ifouse of Lords. The question had assumed such importance that it could no longer be left in the hands of politicians. It had become imperative that the Christian public should declare its opinion, for should Parliament throw out or practically destroy the Bill on the alleged ground that the country was not in favour of dealing with the question, they would feel, had they neglected to give voice to their views, that they had been contributory to the loss of a great measure of justice, designed to heal the direst disease of our country, and to remove the greatest , obstacle of our time to religions progress.

===== 0002601_19080530_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. 11. ROBERTS'S SPEECH.

Ur. HENRY RonF.rtzs, in seconding, said that for many reasons he would have preferred not to have taken a public part in the. discussion of the measure, but when one found the whole nation divided into two camps, in one of which at least there was no selfish thought, no desire for personal aggrandisement, no object of money saving, and on the other side the opposite, then one had a duty to one's convictions and to the cause with which one had been associated practically for a life-time, and he had no option but to take hislittle part in promoting what he believed to be the greatest and most complete measure of temperance reform which had ever been offered to the people of this country. He did not remember any measure that had provoked in the first instance so much rancorous hostility, and in regard to which there had been so remarkable a change of opinion in such a .comparatively short time. The more the measure was understood the more people of moderate views were inclined to its support. It was a very curious thing that those who were most anxious to impress upon the people that this was not a temperancei measure were not temperance people, and one was inclined to look some xhat doubtfully upon the expres- sion of such opinions coming from thoce whose interests lay in maintaining things as they were. It was true they could not make a man sober by Act of Parliament, but it was also true that they could by Act of Parliament make it extremely diffi- cult for a man to get drunk. He remembered the time when there could not be a public holiday with- out it being disgraced by tits excessive amount of drunkenness that occurred. He walk glad to be able to say that there had been a vast improvement in that respect during the last 15 or 20 years, and now it was delightful to notice how rationally our people enjoyed themselves on public holidays and the moderation which they practisA. At the same time one of the saddest things he knew was to see workiag men on a Saturday afternoon, who, with faces blackened with the toil of the workshop and furnace, were unable to reach home without getting drunk and spending a large proportion of those hard-earned wages on whlch their wives and families depended for food and comfort. Many of those men never intended to discredit themselves In this manner, but the temptations which met there in so many iplaces on their way from their work overcame their will power, with the results to which he had referred. The

===== 0002601_19080530_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
TEMPTATIONS TO EXCMSIVE DRINKM

would ho enormously reduced by this Bill and therefore it was a temperance measure. The Bill was not a measure of confiscation, as it was said to be. The 1001 Act for the first time created a vested legal interest in licences. So great an injustice to the people of thia country was that Act that the present Government might have simply reverted to the legal position as it existed prior to 1901, but in the interests of fairness and equity they chose a more excellent way and had agreed to the continuance of one of its main principles for 14 years. It was quite true that an enormous amount of money had been lost in con- nection with the exploitation of public-houses, but it was also true that no money had been lost in capital value since this Bill had been under consideration. He believed something like CO million pounds sterling had been lost in the values of brewery shares and stocks since the time when brewery companies were exploited and capital values enormously inflated, but the whole of that loss occurred with the full knowledge of those who sold their businesses that it would occur. The statements made in regard to confiscation were absolutely untrue. As Mr. Yonge had said, the Bill would aboli.sh 00,000 licences in 11 years, and that in automatic, annual compulsory instalments. He (Mr. Roberts) welcomed that feature of the Bill as one of the grandest propositions ever offered to them, and if they let the chance of securing it go by default their disgrace would be infinite. As regarded clubs, he confessed he had not much sympathy with a lot of the cant which had been uttered about them.

POLITICAL CLCBS AND SOCIAL CLUBS

were a necessity of the times, but the club which was formed for the purpose mainly of the sale of drink was a menace to society, an intolerable nuisance which ought nav3r to have been permitted, and was a most unfair source of competition to the legitimate licensed victuallers and at the same time free from that police supervision to which every publican was subjected. lie could never understand why a man should not be required to observe the same standard of conduct in a club as the law enforced in public-houses. He hoped sincerely that the Government would suppress with a strong hand all those drinking clubs which at present existed, and make it impossible for others to be established when the present Bill became law. He was sorry also that grocers' licences and off-licences were not included in the purview of the Bill as at prosent drafted, but doubtless in the Committee stages of the Bill all those questions would be suitably dealt with. Mr. Roberts emphasised the desirability of securing the support of that large proportion of moderate Con- liervatives whom he knew were fully prepared to give such support to the Bill in its main principles. NucToitiA THE -GOOD.

For over CO years she occupied the foremost place in the world's history. Under her beneficent sway the people of these islands prospered far beyond compare with other nations. Her statesmen knew 'when to take occasion by the hand and make the bonds of freedom wider yet.' The Empire which now owns the sovereignty of her son, has expanded until its shores are kissed by every sea, and the well-beins of innumerable peoples lies in thehands of our legislators. Our children and our children's children will inherit the possessions of a great imperial race, to whom, I believe, Providence has vouchsafed the privileges that attach to the pioneers of progress. Let us see to it that so far as in us lies those who follow us shall be able to testify that we, having done our share in removing the stumbling blocks begotten of the eecessive facilities fol. drinking which now obtain, out of their way have the better fitted them to enter into their great reponsibilities and to add fresh lustre to the British Crown.

THE FREE CHURCHES' STAND FOR T:IE PEOPLE Councillor HNITTHORN, in supporting the resolution, said they were passing through a great moral effort towards more sober living, and this was a time when the voice of the Churches should be heard. in that great battle between tho highest, social, intellectual and moral interests of the people of England on the one hand and the money interests and commercial privileges of a dangerous monopoly on the other, the Free Churches stood for the people. Taken as a whole the Nonconformist Churches of our land stood not merely free from State control and endowment, but Lee from liquor control and domination. There was the more necessity for them to emphasise their views on behalf of the measure in Warrington in view of the desertion of the temperance cause by clergymen, from whom they might have expected some light and leading in the matter. The Government had shown considerable courage in

OPENING OUT A NEW DISTRICT.

The Helsby Parochial Committee resolved that a direct road from Helsby to Ellesmere Port would have the effect of opening out the intervening district for building pur- poses, would be of great commercial benefit to a large portion of the community and would tend to popularize portions of the Chester and Runcorn rural arears as residential neighbourhoods. That the Rural District Council be urged to invite the County Councils of the divisions interested and representatives from the Urban Rural and Parish Councils through whose areas such a road would pass or whose area or parish would be immediately affected thereby, to attend a conference with a view to discussing ways and means of making such a road.

===== 0002601_19080530_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
The Cleavage of Parties.

TO TITE EDITOR, 64 Ex k.:\IINER." Sir,—The days of mourning for the 10S3 of my youthful collaborator in the production of these letters being ended I must return to my allotted task alone. During the period of my silence I have noted the strange cleavage of parties in which the opponents of the Licensing Bill seemed to be overwhelmed with anxiety for the good of the Trade and of those who have invested money in it and have entirely lost sight of the national good. To them it is sterling honesty to oppose the Bill. Our local clergy of the Established Church have declared for and still remain on the side of the Brewer and his Trade : they show no sign of the return of reason and commonsense and indicate no desire to forward national sobriety and national well- being. If the Bill is defective surely there are ways of improving it and making it a better one. Will it go down to the succeeding ages that in the" history of our own times " one party in the State attempted a great moral reform while the other party sulked in their tents and not only would not aid but (lid all they could to prevent the success of the measure ? Is Warrington going to allow it to be said in the future that in a great crisis in the moral history of England the Estab- lished clergy were opposed to reform whilst the Nonconformist clergy were equally unanimous in its favour ? The walls of our town are at present decorated with two bills, one speaks of" Honesty winning " and the other speaks of the Robber Bill.' I pity the people from the bottom of my heart who are content with the kind of "honesty " here referred to. We are nil admirers of honesty and honest dealing, ancl none of us desire to rob any man of his legitimate rights and privileges. The nation which has been able to rise to the emergency in a great national crisis will,' have no fear on the present occasion, do some- thing to rescue our race from the degrading habit of excessive drinking. We cannot make a sober nation by Act of Parliament, but we may by the judicious blending of moral suasion and wise legislation do a great deal to improve the morals, the sobriety, and the physical condition of our race. If the brewers after they have poured out their money like water to create a monopoly in demoralisation are determined to exact the uttermost farthing of compensation for per- mitting the nation to return to righteousness they have only to convince the majority of the nation that they have a right to it. The nation that abolished slavery and that has always been in the van of liberty and freedom has not yet become so decrepit and feeble that it cannot throw off the present yoke of intoxica- tion which is sapping the material and physical well- being of our race. In conclusion let us not forget the lesson taught in those beautiful lines of Longfellow : " Lives of great men all remind us We can make our lives sublime, And, departing, leave behind us Footprints in the sands of time ; " I remain, yours &c.,

PHIL. SQUOD.

WHITSUNTIDE EXCURSIONS.

The excursions arranged by the London and North- Western Railway for the Whitsuntide holidays are very numerous and attractive, and should meet the demand of all who are desirous of visiting friends or who may ha on pleasure bent. The list covers many parts of England, Scotland, Ireland and Wale., and to some of the most prominent of these we would call attention. To those intending to visit Ireland, it will be well known that the Ncrth-Weatern Company's route to the Emerald Isle is the most popular and expeditious, the journey being made by the short sea route via Holyhead. To Greenore, and the North of Ireland, many cheap trips have Wen fixed, and Greenore, it may be said, is a convenient centre for excursions on Carlinford Lough, and through the Mourne mountains district, and other Points. Those desiring one of the most delightful holidays should visit Greener°, and make this a centre. There is excellent hotel accommodation under the management of the Company. Golf links (18 hole course) and Club House have been provided, and resi- dents in the hotel have free use of same. There is also excellent sea and river fishing. Excursions to London are also fixed on various dates, and on June Gth to York, ScarboroYand other stations North-Eastern dis- trict, June sth to West of England by the direct and popular route, via the Severn Tunnel. June sth South and Central Wales, South Coast watering places and the Eastern counties. Excursions to Scotland, all parts, on June sth, 10th and 11th, on which trains through carri- ages will be run to Glasgow and Edinboro'. The local tripper is also well catered for and many special tripe are arranged to Blackpool, North Wales line, Liverpool, Manchester, Huddersfield, Leeds, &c. The week-end, short and long date tickets usually issued on Fridays sud Saturdays will be issued on June sth and Gth, the short date tickets being available for return any day up to and including the following Wednesday and the longer period any day up to and including the following Monday week. The Great Eastern Railway Co., announce Hitt reduced fares available for eight days will be issued to Brussels via Harwich and Antwerp. Passengers leaving the Northern and Midland Counties in the afternoon reach Brussels next morning after a comfortable night's rest on board the steamer. For visiting the Hague Scheveningen (the Dutch Brighton) and Amsterdam for the cities of the Zlyder Zee, special facilities are offered via the Great Eastern Railway's British Royal Mail Harwich-Hook of Holland route. From the Hook of Holland through carriages and restaurant cars run on the express trams to Cologne, Bale and Berlin. The Cheshire Lines Company will issue cheap excur- sion tickets from Warrington to Douglas. EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

ST. PETER'S GUILD SALE OF WORK.

On Saturday afternoon, the members of the St. Peter's Children's Guild held their sale of work in the schoolroom, Newton. Mrs. Pennington' in opening the sale of work, spoke of the work of the Guild. The Vicar, the Rev. J. W. Ryder, who presided, explained that the work was done by the children of their school, and spoke in praise of the work done. The articles were speedily sold, and a good amount was realized for the Guild funds. COLLIERY FArALITY. IIAVDOCK MAN'S SAD END.

PRESENTATIONS TO VIADUCT FORMIEN.

Last Friday night a presentation took place at. the Viaduct Institute, to three retiring foremen—Mr. Clare, Mr. Barcroft, and Mr. Cave —who retired owing to ago limit, after a long period of service with the London and North-Western Railway Comparo, at the Waggon Works, Earlestown. The presents which had been subscribed for by the foremen, inspectors, and a few friends, were of a very appro- priate character, consisting of a handsome chiming I clock for Mr. Clara, a smoker's cabinet for Mr. Barcroft, and a handsome cruet and a barometer for Mr. Cave. The chair was taken by Mr. C. W. Emmett, assistant superintendent, who was supported by Mr. Emmett and Mr. T. Ormand, Mr.Sinalley and Mr. Goff. Mr. Bmmett and Mr.Ormand eulogized the excellent services rendered by the three gentlemen whom they had met to honour that evening, saying they had discharged their duties I faithfully to the company and with advantage to the servants. Mr. Goff followed, and spoke of the long connection between himself and Mr. Clare, whilst occupying the position of junior foreman, and hoped that Mr. Clare and the others would remember those they left behind. The Chairman called upon Mr. B. Harrison to perform the pleasing duty of handing the presents to the recipients. Mr. Harrison spoke of their long career as foremen,a period extending over 30 years, and recalled many incidents in their lives,concluding by presenting the articles with a few well chosen words to each one as he handed them their presents. Messrs. Clare, Barcroft, and Cave each returned thanks. Mr. Smalley proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman and subscribers, which was seconded by Mr. Bolton. Ths. arrangements were in the hands of Mr. Smalley and Mr. Goff, chief foremen of the Waggon Shop and Smithies respectively.

ST. HELENS.

INCREASE IN ST. HELENS RATES.

The Finance Committee of the St. Helens Tow Council on Wednesday, decided to increase the borough rate by 3d. in the pound. There will probably be an increase of Id. in the poor rate. ST. HELENS CANAL TRAGEDY.

The body of the young woman found in the S. Helens Canal on Monday was on Tuesday identified as that of Elizabeth M'Quaid, single woman, aged twenty who had left her home only about an hour before her hat and umbrella were found in the canal. She lived with her widowed mother in Wilfred-street, and two years ago was sent to Rainhill Asylum, and was an Inmate of that institution for nine months. On her discharge she went to several places of service. A week ago she left a situation and had since complained of pains in her head. When she left home at two o'clock on Monday afternoon she said to her young sister, " If I don't get a place, goodness knows when you will see me again."

TOO MUCH ELEVATED.

OLD SWEEP'S SUNDAY PRANK.

William M`Cawley, a St. Helens labourer, created great amusement at the police court, on Monday, when he was charged with disorderly conduct. M`Cawley when charged, said, "I went to try and clean the chimney-pot. We were smoked out of tho house, and could not clean it. It had got full of soot." Police-constable Marmot said that at ten minutes past three on Sunday afternoon he was called from the Town Hall to prisoner, who seas on tho roof of his house in Claughton-street. M`Cawley was clinging to a chimney-stack. After a lot of persuasion he got the man to come down, and found that he was drunk. _

The Clerk : Ho W did you get him down ? Witness said he got down from roof to roof, and he was nearly falling off two or three times. When he reached the ground he said he could not reach the chimney top, and wanted to take a stool and go up again. Prismaer said that was correct. The Chairman : You are not a chimney-sweep? Prison : I used to be ten years ago. Since then I have been in Pilkington's collieries, and 1 have to go inside the boilers to clean them.

He was fined ss. and costs or seven days. SERIOUS ST. HELENS CASE.

Thomas Hallwood, a St. Helens collier, who was _arrested a week ago on a charge of attempting suicide, was brought before the magistrates at St. Helens on Saturday, when it was stated that the man, after drink- ing all day, took out his razor and terrified his wife by attempting to cut his throat. She struggled and took the razor from him. Ho had been in custody a week, and now stated that he had taken the pledge, and he was discharged on promising not to repeat the offence.— John Colligan, a St. llelens labourer, who had deserted his wife and three children in October last, and caused the guardians considerable expense, was dealt with by the St. Helens magistrates on Saturday. It was stated that this was the third time Colligan had run away, and be had been in regular work in Rochdale, whilst his wife and children had been in the workhouse. He was sent to prison for two months' hard labour.—John Derbyshire, a St. Helens hawker, who was arrested a the previous night for threatening to kill his children, was on'Saturday sent by the St. Ilelens magistrates for fourteen days' detention.—Dr. Latham certified that he was not in his right mind.

MOTORIST HAUL.

James Boyle Livesey, a Blackburn merchant, who resides at Southport, when charged at St. Helens County Petty Sessions, on Tuesday, with exceeding the speed limit on the Warrington road at Bold, replied, " I plead guilty to twenty-seven miles an hour.' Superintendent Baxendale said that inconsequence of complaints Sergeant Turner and a constable timed motors for a quarter-mile on the Bold road. On May 18 they found that Mr. Livesey covered the quarter-mile in two-seconds, which made twenty-eight miles an hour. Livesey had been fined £3 at Lytham last year, and the magistrates now said they would repeat that penalty. Walter Ormerod, company director, admitted that he covered a quarter-mile on the Mossborough road between Knowsley and Rainford at a speed of over thirty-one miles an hour.—He was fined £3 and costs. Thomas Gregory, schoolmaster, of St. Helens, and Ralph K. Milne, solicitor, were charged with driving motor-cycles at over twenty-three miles an hour at Windle Flat, on the Rainford road on Sunday, the 10th inst.

Mr. thloe pointed out that the excess of speed alleged by the police was very slightly over the limit. He was fined 10s. and costs.

MR. C. E. PARKER AND LEATHER TRADE

FEDERATION.

In the Manchester, .Liverpool and District Tanners' Federation Year Book, which has just been issued for 1908, Mr. Charles E. Parker has an article headed " Federate ! Federate ! ! Federate I! ! " He says : —The Federation of the Tanners of the United Kingdom in their common interests makes sure, if somewhat slow progress. The very successful gathering held in the Midland Hotel, London, on November 7th, 1907, was a visible sign of this. Over one hundred tanners representing almost every import- ant hide-tanning firm in Great Britain and Ireland met in response to the invitation of the three existing Federations. There was a distinct evidence of a desire to draw together for common action,a recogni- tion that if tho trade does not hang together, many of its members may have to hang singly. The principal subjects discussed were as fellows :—"The Contracting Out Clause in the Federation of the Trade," "Tanning Material Contracts,"" Private Collections of Hides and their Influence on Auction Marts," and "The Adoption of the new I.A.L.T.c. Method of Analysis." Since this gathering the executives of the Federations have not been idle, but have been seeking to bring all tanners into association, and progress has been made. Although the Federations already in existence have been able to accomplish much for their members, and indeed for the whole trade, we are constantly coming up against subjects which cannot be put on a right basis without the co-operation and support of the whole trade. Take the somewhat simple question of horns, about which Mr. William Harvey writes. Not a single reason can be brought forward, not a single advantage suggested in favour of bringing horns into a tannery and their ?ending them out again. All admit that in carriage, labour, &c., the custom involves an unnecessary and foolish annual waste, and yet this will go on and on for over unless as a united trade we decline to buy what we have no use for, and so induce the hide markets to deal with horns and tails as they do with fat, &c. The executives of the existing Federations clearly recog- LIIZO the limits to collective action, and have no desire fo interfere with, or dictate to, tanners in those spheres to action in which individual freedom is desirable and necessary ; but everyone will admit that there are sub- jects which are of common interest, both in the direc- tion of reform and of defence which can only he effectively dealt with by a federation of the Whole trade. It will be for the tanners themselves in their collective capacity wisely to determine where collective action is wise and profitable, and where it is desirable to leave individual action unfettered. a The Year Book is replete with special articles on topics connected with the trade, and dealt with by experts in readable and instructive form. There are also five art illustrations of Dr. J. Gordon Parker, the late Mr. Jonathan L. Evans, Mr. Robert Mellor, J.P., the late Mr. P. E. Sparko Evans, Mr. Stanley George Lutwyche. The book may be had from Mr. W. B. Morton, The Mount, Penketh, who has also copies for 1906 and 1907.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

Cricket. At last Warrington have achieved a victory. Let U 3 hope it may be the precursor or a long series of triumphs, for before Saturday there had been nothing in the club's performances to arouse enthusiasm. The Warringtonians did well in disposing of Prescot on their o Arn ground, and that without maah ditßoulty. Delightful weather favoured the fixture and Warring. ton had the advantage of batting first on a capital pitch. J. W. Bibby made his fimt appearance this season, and needless to say was heartily welcomed. T. Wright was unable to appear, owing to an injured hand. Going to the wicket first the Warriugtonians made 121, B. Silcock, the captain, distinguishing himself with a carefully compiled 28.

-- The Prescot innings realized only 76. 11. Callon and Cresswell were in great form with the ball, Callon accounting for 5 wickets for 17 runs while the profes- sional secured 4 for 23. The Warrington fielding was brilliant.

Warrington play their first home match, when Preston will visit the Arpley enclosure.

-- The second team achieved another splendid victory at the expense of Prescot second. The Warringtonians compiled 163, and tumbled out the visitors for 65. A. Thewlls gave a brilliant display with the bat, his 49 including eight 4's and two 3's. P. B. Ilawtin knocked up 41 in faultless style, while Houghton made a capital 23. W. Sephton (23) was the only visiting player to reach double figures. W. Bennett captured six wickets for 26 runs, whilst Thewlis bagged three for 33.

-- In the Warrington and District League Rylands Bros. tried conclusions with Cheshire Lines, and suffered defeat by the narrow margin of O. J. McKay bowled splendidly for the visitor; taking seven wickets for six runs, and McGowan secured eight for 10.

Wilderspool Brewery defeated Bewsey Wesleyans by 37 to 21. The match was exciting, for at one time Bewsey had lost five wickets without securing a run. R. Hallows for Wilderspool took nine wickets for 14 runs, and performed the " hat trick."

-- St. James' registered a victory over Walker's brewery, making 63 for 9 while the Brewery men were all out for 64. J. Hallam (24) and C. Holt (23) batted in grand style for the victors and E. Allen made an excellent 22 for the brewery.

An eleven representing the Warrington and District League will play the North Liverpool League on the Corporation Electric Supply Ground, Lister Drive, Green-lane, Liverpool, on Whit Monday morn- ing. The following team has been selected to do duty for Warrington P. Foster (captain) and J. Cartwright (St. Jernes'), J. Duckworth (vice-captain) (Cheshire Lines Engineers), R. Hallows and W. Howard (Waders- pool Brewery), C. Burrell and J. Woodhead ("Guadian" Office),J. Armitage and R. Craddock (Bervsey Wesleyans), G. McGowan and E. P. Sanders ( Rylands' Bros.); with W. Hill (Cheshire Lines Engineers), S. Minett (Bemsey Wesleyans),J. Layfleld (itylands' Bros.) and T. Thompson (Wilderspool Brewery), reserves; umpire, Mr. S. J. Thompson.

In the Warrington Junior League, Marahhouss-llne defeated Orford by 11 runs, not a single player reaching double figures. Sankey inflicted a very severe drubbing on St. Mary's, the most successful batsman being J. Bradburn who knocked up 51 in grand style. There was a close contest between St. James's and Lnvely- lane, the latter winning by 4 runs. C. F. SlsTbury was the top scorer with 13.

I--

Latchford Baptists visited Winwick Asylum and inflicted a severe defeat on the villagers. The home team batted first, amassing 58, against which the visitors replied with a total of 191. li'or the victors J. Conway bowled well, taking 6 wickets for 18 runs. Elliot Harris, of Rugby football fame, batted well, being top scorer with 59 to his credit. J. Stowell also batted in good style, making 32 not out.

Grappenhall entertained Glazebury and were victorious by 56. E. Banks and W. Carter each knocked up 13, and J. Warburton captured 8 wickets for 13 runs.

Warrington Police have got a splendid team this season and on Wednesday they triumphed over the Salford custodians of tha peace by 82 and 33. Dr. Smith knocked up an excellent 21 and the Chief Constable was also in good form, his innings realizing 19. Green and Dr. Smith bowled well, the first named taking five wickets for 5 runs.

In the absence of W. H. Podmore, through injury, his deputy, T. G. Bate, took charge of the Warrington Thursday team this week when the game with Hoole was played on the Arpley enclosure under the most delightful weather conditions, and on a splendid wicket. .Runs came freely from the start and the visitors made the creditable total of 122. Per the home side J. F. Foster and J. H. Constable had the game well in hand when the time rule stoppsi play, having made 66 for no wickets.

Walker's Brewery met Widnes Police at Widnes on Thursday and the game resulted in an easy win for the visitors. For the winners J. Milner batted well,making 34 before being caught. A. Holland also bowled splen- didly, taking 7 wickets for twice as many runs. Walker's innings totalled 35 while Widnes were all dis- missed for 39.

There is no mistaking the fact that of late cyclists have been receiving a fair share of fortune's favour The glorious weather of Saturday last induced about 20 members of the Warrington Cycling Club to make the Journey to Delamere, and needless to sly the ride to what is claimed by many as bEiag the most delightful part of Cheshire, was greatly enjoyed by all taking part. After tea, which was partaken of at that quaint old hostelry the Abbey Arms, the rem .n journey was com- menced, the members by way of a little deviation travelling fora few miles by the forest path, home being reached in good time.

-- Oh Tuesday evening, Glazebury was the destination and nearly a score of members putting in an appearance, a capital evening was spent, an impromptu concert by the club's reserve contingent being much enjoyed.

The first bowling handicap of the season, which carries with it the Silver Challenge Cup, was com- menced on Wednesday evening on the Club Green at Sankey. A record entry has been received and some excellent games were witnessed. The holder of the Cup, Mr. W. Unsworth, was defeated in the first round. The games will be continued each Wednesday evening until the haudicap is completed.

To-day the Club travel to Che-iter, the old city with its many attractions always having been a favourite resort of the Warringtonians and a good muster of members is confidently anticipated.

For the benefit of those not having dilly acquaint- ance with the Warrington bridge, it mty interest thorn to know that they can now cross over into Cheshire with much less risk to their limbs and machines than was previously the case,a few lours suMcin,g to remedy what has been a source of constant danger fo: weeks.

WYCLIFFE CHURCH. -- Annual Sale of Work

The annual sale of work in connection with Wycliffe Congregational Church was held in the assembly raom of the Wycliffe Memorial Buildings on Thursday after- noon when there wss a large attenianee. The sale xvas opened by Mrs. Ziba Armitage and the Rev. John Yonge presided. The following is a list of the stall-holders : Fancy Work Stall :N-Mrs. Armitage. Mrs. Yonge, Mrs. Garland, Mrs. E. 'S. Garland and Miss Walker. Plain Work Stall :—Mrs. Walker, Mrs. Slack, Mrs. Winskill, Mrs. Jeffery and Mrs. Wallington. Refreshment Stall ;—Mrs. Leiper, Mrs. Brooks, Misses Annie, Florence and Agnes Wallington, Miss Harrison, Miss Porritt, Misses Hawthorn, Misses Roberts, Miss Corney, Miss Wood, Miss Bateman, Miss Beswick and Miss Wnlmsley. The CIIAIRM AN said he was sure that they were all pleased to have Mrs. Ziba Armitage with them that afternoon. The object of the sale was to raise money for the variaus purposes which Wycliffe Church stood for. Last year he believed they raised about £lOO and they were hopeful of again raising that amount. They were all pleased to see members of other churches with them. Mrs. ARMITAGF: said that four different ladies had been approached to open the sale of work but they had beenonsuccessful in obtaining the services of any of those ladies. So Mr. Yonge had told her that she had better do it. That was why she was there. She believed in brevity. She had once attended a sale of

work in Warrington when the opening ceremony lasted an hour so that she had only an hour to spend money in, and she hid some of it with her when she left. There were two competitions : one for the best made article which brought the largest price—the material not to have cost more than a shilling—and one with similar conditions, only the price of the material had not to be more thin sixpence. In the shilling competition Miss Elarrisoa had won the first prize ; Mrs. Wilker the second ; and Miss Slack the third, whilst in the sixpenny competition Mrs. Cadman was first, Mrs. Thoznpsonsecond, and Miss Lo we,thi rd. (Applause.) Mr. Ci.WALLINGTON proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Armitage. Mr. W. FL PAYTON seconded the motion, which was carried unanimously. From 3 to 6 o'clock an organ recital was given in the Cinrch by Mr. W. 11. Payton and was much appreciated During the evening there were half-hour concerts, the programme including piano duet by Miss Farrar and Mr. W. H.Payton, songs by Miss Mary Hawthorn, Miss J. Armstrong' Miss Grths, Mr. B. Hibbert, Mr. Levi Richardson, Mr. Paroy Bailey, duet by Mr. Levi Richardson and Miss Avioe Beswick, recitation by Miss Carney and reading by Mr. H. M. Ashton. The proceeds of the sale amounted to between 670 and £3O. We are informed that a further sale of surplus goods will be held In the autumn.

WARRINGTON -ASSESSIENT COMMITTEE.

Important Appea Is.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Assessment Com nittee was held at the Workhouse yesterday morn- ing. There were present :—Messrs. J. Billinge,W. Gill IV. Furness, G. Mather, J. C. Sutton (clerk), R. R. Meacock (valuer), W. El. Woodcook (assistant overseer). Pending the election of chairman the Clerk presided. He remarked that Mr. A. J. Jolley had for some years past heen the chairman and Mr. J. Billinge the vice- chairman.

ELECTION OF CHAIRMAN. Mr. A. BitooKs said he was sure that if Mr. Jolley_ were present or even if it had not been for his continued absence from meetings of the Guardians, the com- mittee would be unanimously in favour of re-appointing him. In his absence there was nothing for it but to appoint some other gentleman and he had therefore pleasure in proposing the election of Mr. Billinge. • Mr. MATH ER seconded and Mr. Gm. supported. The CLERK paid a tribute to the services of Mr.Jolley, remarking that a more efficient chairman of an Assess- ment Committee it would be impossible to find in Poor Law experience. While paying that respect to the late chairman, he welcomed Mr. Billing° who was a courteous and efficient gentleman and, he was sure, would do honour to the office. It would be a most important year, for subject to any amicable arrangement that might be arrived at between the valuer of the b,ig firma of Warrington, there would undoubtedly be great opposition to important increases of assessments. The resolution was unanimously agreed to. The CHAIRMAN said he looked favourably on the prospects for the future because he felt that when the ratepayers became conversant with the arrangements of the committee for the revaluation of the whole of the Union quinquenially they would realize that they were based on principles of equity and would be quite satisfied. Mr. A. Bnootts was elected vice-chairman and the Chairman and Clerk were appointed representatives on a committee of representatives of Lancashire Union Assessment Committees and Overseers. FIRTH COMPANY'S ASSESSMENT. Mr. RATLIBONE (slocetary) appealed on behalf of the Firth Co. Ltd., wire manafacturers, against the assess- ment of their works at £1,002 gross and £822 rateable. lie considered that an increase from £317 to £321 was very excessive. As a cotton factory full of machinery and more fully occupied than it was at present, the works was only assessed at £3lB net. A portion of their machinery had not been running for some time and the only increase in the buildings was a small shed. They employed about 200 hands. The wire trade to-day i was n a very difficult position. In Warrington they were paying the highest rate of wages of any wire dis- trict in the country, independent of the competition they had to meet from abroad. The Committee decided to adjourn their decision pending an interview between the valuer and the firm.

PEARSON AND KNOWLES COAL AND IRON CO LTD. Mr. JAMES DODDS (secretary) appealed on behalf of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., against the assessment of their works at £10,902 grass and £7,207 ratable and of the works formerly occupied by Messrs. Smith in Dallam Lane at £276 gross and £232 rateable. He pointed out that the increase in vahi- ation was more than 50 per cent. Under the new valuation made in 1900 the basis of assessment was £2,560 for Bewsey, and £2,060 for Dallam, a total of £1,620. Since then they had made certain extensions in regard to whifh they had met the valuer and taking the additional assessments on the basis of the valuation of 1900 it should make a total of £1,761. The new assess- , ment, however, was .£7,207. They did not understand how such figures were arrived at and he asked for an adjournment in order that the matter might be gone into before formally appealing. The CHAIRMAN : You feel that the valuer has rather over-estimated? that he has not allowed sufficient for wear and tear? Mr. DODDS : We are certain of that and there are • reasons which we can put before you which will prove that we are not in beneficial occupation of a large part of the works for which we are assessed. In answer to the Chairman, Mr. WooneOCK said the works occupied 23 acres.

MESSRS. RYLANDS BROS. LTD. Mr. THOMAS LESI.IE Invia is similar propo3ition on behalf of Messrs. Ryland s Bros. Ltd., whose new assess- ment was £4,401 gross and £3,325 rateable(Church-street) and £5,363 gross,and £3,925 rateable (Dalton Bank).He said tho increase was over 100 per cent. The last valua- tion was made in 1930, and since then there had been certain additions. The total of the old valuation was £3,170 10s. and the new one was £7,259. The works, he admitted were fully occupied. . _

Mr. W6ODCOCK said par'i, of the Dalton Bink premises had not been assessed by the overseers, and ho supposed that would flow be included in the assessment, and would account for some of the increase.

It was decided to adjourn both cases .for conferences. between the valuer and the firms.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

The best thing at the Palace and Hippodrome this, week is undoubtedly the clever Kauffman Troupe. These eight lady cyclists are unquestionably the finest expert and trick riders that have ever been seen in Warrington. Indeed it is open to question whether there are any cleverer exponents of skilful cycling before the public at the present time. Not only are these ladies dainty and graceful in all they do, bet their movements when riding together are a marvel of complicated evolutions which take one's breath away in the narrow margin of safety which is left within the limits of We stage. Suffice it to say that the nightly appearance of the Kauffinans has been the signal for most vociferous cheering and manifest enthusiasm, and the " recalls , have been unusually numerous. The Will Douglas . Troupe of Five Jolly Bakers is another conspicuous success, some acrobat feats and contortionist displays. hawing the stamp of novelty and exceeding smartness: writ large upon them. The daring performances of Alfonso Silvan° as an equilibrist are loth marvellous and startling, and hold the audience spellbound. The . feat of delicate balancing during which a mandolino is played is made vastly more difficult when s lighted -paraffin lamp has to be maintained on the head throughout all that is done. - The novel performance of the Mayvilles in the shape of miniature figures upon a miniature stage is highly Meritorious, and takes the audience's fancy immensely.; the mimicry of many well-known public entertainers bearing upon it the impress of great originality if not of actual genius. The Sisters Belvoir and Maud - Beaumont contribute some singing which has the merit of animation and rerre, while the inseparable Selby and Myers greatly amuse the audience with the absolute uniformity of their twin-like movements and dancing broken up, as it is, with smart ancl sparkling patter. Altogether the Palace show of this week is distinctly superior, and well worth a visit. NEXT WEEK'S PROCUZAIIIIE. , Another capital programme is announced for next : week, Tha bill" is topped by Little Carl, eccentric comedian, from the Empire London, and another great attraction will be provided by the Ten Ji Troupe of wonderful Japanese magicians. The TJannons, vocal duettists and champion high pedestal dancers of the world, will appear. Will Lannon is the winner and •: holder of the champion belt and is still willing to ad-side. eThe f who c e any il, a n world, oonm ae directpedesta from the Oxford for£ 50h Oxford and Tivoli,London,will present their comedy a 4,: "The policeman and the maid." Other contributors to the programme will be : —Ridiculous Reeco, burlesque acrobat ; Lieutenant Travis, ventriloquist ; Mdlle Maydee's miniature vaudeville theatre ; Marion Scotts dainty commedienne.

THE EXAMINER. MAY 30th, 1908.

Recruit : "Please, sergeant, I've got a splinter in my 'and." Sergeant-Instructor : "Wot yer been doin' ? Strokin' yer 'ead ?"—" Punch." ' * * The new Bolton School at Latchford was auspici- ously opened on Thursday, notwithstanding that the proceedings smacked somewhat of Hamlet without the Prince, seeing that Alderman Bolton was conspicuously absent. Rumour hath it that he was visiting a famous orchid show, and it is conceivable that the Alderman's passion for orchids is greater than his enthusiasm for education. Alderman Pierpoint made an• efficient chairman and in a chatty, commonsense speech he pictured Latchford as it was in 1855, and noted the changes in the once essentially rural village. Alderman Ellison followed snit, and harked back to such a •remote past as 1811, prior to which there was neither school nor place of worship in Latchford. Now the " village " is provided with a handsome, commodious, Council school—which will soon be hidden away by rows of cottages already springing up with mushroom-like rapidity. The Balton School possesses a splendid site badly spoiled ! •* * * Tact is good taste in action.—Than. If a clever man commit a folly, it is no small one. —Goethe. No entertainment is so cheap' as reading, nor any pleasure so lasting.—Montague. A tombstone is often a monument of lies above the one Ivho lies beneath.-11. 11. Sheridan. I would hang all misers by the heels, that their money might ran out of their pockets. —Rowland Hill. All that mankind has done, thought, gained or been, it is lying as in magic preservation in the pages of books. —Carlyle. They who marry where they do not love will love where they do not marry.—Faller. One should take take not to grow too wise for so great a pleasure of life as laughter.—Addison. Dear Sir.—During the past six months we have had some extremely damp and foggy weather, and whilst hoisting in and out ocean tugs, copper punts and other small crafts, I contracted several chills one after the other, which finally settled in my legs and and feet, causing them to swell ; and to make matters worse I had the misfortune to fall out of a submerged torpedo tube,and sprained both ankles. I was placed on the sick list and given the usual naval treatment, which did me no good. Seeing your advertisement, I determined to try your famous GouttPills, and after taking a few doses they worked marvels. All the swelling disappeared and my legs are as strong as ever again.
